Behrami attacca Spence: "È un gregario, si è presentato come un attore di Hollywood. Qua non è l'NBA"

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Behrami attacca Spence: "È un gregario, si è presentato come un attore di Hollywood. Qua non è l'NBA"

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Valon Behrami non risparmia critiche a Djed Spence. L'ex centrocampista, oggi opinionista di DAZN, ha espresso un giudizio molto severo sul giocatore dell'Inter, soffermandosi soprattutto sull'atteggiamento mostrato al momento del suo arrivo in nerazzurro.

Secondo Behrami, Spence si sarebbe presentato con aspettative eccessive rispetto al ruolo che dovrebbe ricoprire all'interno della squadra. Il commentatore ha utilizzato un paragone particolarmente duro con il mondo dello spettacolo e con l'NBA.

LE PAROLE DI BEHRAMI – «Spence? Penso che non ci avrei fatto sicuramente amicizia… Sembra che sia arrivato qui da La La Land o da Hollywood, l'ho visto anche all'inizio, quando è arrivato all'Inter: sembrava un rapper di Hollywood… La sensazione è che qui è un gregario, e invece si sente come la star che tutti aspettavano.

Questo è stato l'atteggiamento iniziale, poi è migliorato, ci ha messo un po' di tempo, ha avuto dei problemi, ma mi sembra che stia rientrando in carreggiata. Di sicuro il primo impatto non è stato positivo: qui non è l'NBA, dove conta più l'intrattenimento che il risultato. Adesso ha la possibilità di dimostrare sul campo quello che si sente addosso, ma è difficile arrivare ai livelli che lui pensa. Non mi piace quando si presentano così. Tornando ai 32,5 come picco di velocità massima: ce ne saranno 100 che hanno raggiunto quel valore ad oggi…».

Per l'ex centrocampista, il problema riguarda soprattutto la distanza tra le aspettative con cui il calciatore si sarebbe presentato e il livello che, a suo giudizio, deve ancora dimostrare di poter raggiungere.

A 17-Million-Line Proof Says Yes. Here Is What It

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On November 20, 2026, Levent Alpöge released a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis, written with Claude, as a Lean library of about 17 million lines. A day later the Lean and Mathlib communities certified both its statement and its proof. Clay's prize still needs publication and time, but as a machine-checked result the question is answered: every nontrivial zero of ζ(s) lies on the line Re(s) = ½.

A proof is a finite object. Lean does not walk the zeros one by one. It checks that every step follows from the last. So this post is not a complaint about size. It asks a different question, one a proof does not answer by itself:

If a zero had left the line, how long would it take to notice?

Map each zero ρ through w = (ρ − 1)/ρ. On the critical line |w| = 1 exactly. The functional equation pairs a zero at σ + it with one at (1 − σ) + it, and their images obey

So if a zero sits at σ > ½, its image falls inside the unit circle and its twin falls outside. In Li's criterion, λₙ = Σ [1 − (1 − 1/ρ)ⁿ], the inside one fades and the outside one grows like |w′|ⁿ. For large t,

The twin's growth eventually pushes some λₙ below zero. That negative value is the signature of an off-line zero.

Growth of Δ/t² per step is very slow, and the twin has to beat something: the on-line zeros, whose combined contribution itself grows as (n/2)(ln n − 1 + γ − ln 2π). Setting the twin equal to that background gives the threshold:

n_crit · Δ / t² ≈ ln n_crit + ln(ln n_crit + c) − ln 4

So the number of Li coefficients you need scales as t² log t / Δ. The t²/Δ part was known (Oesterlé 2000; Voros 2017). The log factor matters, because it means the "constant" in front is not constant. An earlier version of my own paper got this wrong: it claimed a universal value of 29.93. That value fails at every height I tested.

I built λₙ from the first 200 actual zeros of ζ plus the known density of zeros above them. I checked it against the exact λ₁…λ₅, then inserted one hypothetical off-line pair and scanned for the first negative λₙ:

At t = 10¹², the same formula needs about 6 × 10²⁶ coefficients.

The core of the model fits in a few lines:

Here is the part I find interesting. Nothing in that threshold uses the primes. It depends on two things only: the pairing (|w|·|w′| = 1) and how fast the background grows. For L-functions of conductor q, the cost barely moves. On real zeros of L(s, χ₋₄) the shift in n·Δ/t² is about +0.15, not ln 4.

The off-line zeros above were inserted by hand. Some functions don't need that. Davenport and Heilbronn built Dirichlet series with the same kind of functional equation as ζ but no Euler product, and their zeros genuinely wander off the line. I took the partner function mod 5. Its strongest off-line zero sits at 2.3086 + 8.9184i.

Before computing anything exact, I wrote down the prediction: the background for conductor 5, plus the twins of its off-line zeros. Then I computed its Li coefficients exactly, from Taylor coefficients of log Λ around s = 1. That method uses no zeros at all, and two independent runs agree to 10⁻⁸.

No primes in the input, a real off-line zero, and the crossing lands exactly where the pairing says it should.

So the cost of seeing is geometry, and the reason nothing is there to see is arithmetic. The Davenport–Heilbronn function has the geometry without the arithmetic, and its zeros leave the line. The proof settles the second. The threshold describes the first: how far out a twin would have to be, at a given height, before a sign test could see it.

Both kinds of result belong in mathematics. A proof tells you that. A measured invariant tells you how hard it is to look.

Paper, code and data: 10.5281/zenodo.24447722

I vibe coded my own Trello. Drag and drop took two fixes, and the whole thing cost 14 cents.

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A kanban board is the classic "looks easy" app. Lists and cards are simple. Dragging a card from one list to another, dropping it between two others, and having it still be there after a reload is where most first attempts fall over.

So I opened an empty folder, typed one paragraph, and let the agent work. About three and a half minutes later I had Cardflow: three lists with sample cards, new lists and cards, a card editor, coloured labels, and everything saved in the browser. That first version cost 5 cents.

Build a kanban board like Trello, called Cardflow. Three lists to start: To do, Doing, Done, with sample cards. I can add lists and cards, edit a card by clicking it, give it a coloured label, and drag cards between lists and reorder them. Everything is remembered in the browser. Colourful, friendly design with a gradient background. Give it its own name, colours and logo; do not copy any brand. Plain HTML, CSS and JavaScript, no build tools and no installs.

Three things in that prompt did most of the work:

The drag and drop needed two more requests, and I think that is the honest part of this story.

After that second fix it told me what its test was worth: it had simulated the drag in code, not with a real mouse, so "a real mouse drag in the Preview is still worth a quick try". I tried it. It worked.

With both fixes the clock was at about six minutes and the cost at 14 cents.

Add a due date to cards, and show overdue cards with a red badge.

Forty seconds later the card editor had a date field, and a card that was late wore a red badge.

It is a real, working board for one person in one browser. It is not Trello: no accounts, no sharing, no sync between devices, no attachments. For a personal board, a prototype, or the start of a real project, that is exactly the part you want done in minutes.

I used VibeCode.surf, a desktop AI code editor built around agents: they write the code, run it, and test it in a real browser on your own computer, with any model you choose. It runs on Mac, Windows and Linux and starts with a 7-day free trial.

Copy the prompt above, change the name, and see what yours looks like.

AI Hallucination in Specs: The 15-Minute Claim Trace Audit

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 22 • Originally published at specky.space

AI-written specs sound sourced but often aren't. The Claim Trace is a 15-minute audit that labels every claim Sourced, Inferred, Assumed or Invented before engineering or a coding agent builds from it.

AI hallucination in specs is when an AI-written PRD or spec states something about your users, market or product that no source supports, in the same confident voice as the parts that are true. The fix is a short audit before anyone builds: trace every claim and label it Sourced, Inferred, Assumed or Invented. We call that audit the Claim Trace, and it takes about 15 minutes.

Spec hallucination is a specific kind of what NIST calls confabulation: "confidently stated but erroneous or false content" that can mislead the reader (NIST AI 600-1). In a spec, it lives in the Problem, Context and Success Metrics sections, the parts that are supposed to come from evidence.

It rarely looks like a made-up feature. The feature is plausible and the structure is clean. The invented part is the justification:

Each reads like a finding. None may be one. The model has read thousands of PRDs, so it produces the shape of evidence: a behaviour, a segment, a direction of change. If your inputs were thin, it fills the gaps with what is statistically typical for a spec like yours.

Researchers split hallucinations into two types (Ji et al., 2022), and both show up in specs:

Extrinsic hallucinations are the harder ones to catch, because there is nothing to compare them against. The reader has to notice that a sentence has no source, which is exactly what fluent writing hides.

A 2025 paper by researchers at OpenAI and Georgia Tech argues that models hallucinate because training and evaluation reward guessing over admitting uncertainty, much like a student guessing on a multiple-choice exam (Kalai et al., 2025). Of 10 widely used benchmarks the authors reviewed, 9 grade answers strictly right or wrong and give no credit for "I don't know". Under that scoring, a confident guess always beats an honest blank.

The paper also explains why some facts are more exposed than others. If 20% of a type of fact (their example is people's birthdays) appears only once in the training data, you should expect a base model to hallucinate on at least 20% of those facts. When the authors asked one large open model for a birthday and said "only answer if you know", it gave three different wrong dates.

Now apply that to your spec. Facts about your customers, your churned accounts and your admins' weekly habits appear zero times in any training data. The only way a model can state them is if you supplied them. Anything specific about your users that you did not provide is, by construction, a guess dressed as a finding.

NIST adds one more warning that matters for product documents: generated text may include confabulated logic or citations that justify the answer, which makes readers more likely to trust it (NIST, 2024). A spec that says "as customers told us in Q3" is more persuasive than one that just asserts, whether or not the Q3 conversation exists.

Drafting cost collapsed. A spec used to take a PM a day, and that day forced contact with the evidence. Now the draft arrives in under a minute, before anyone has reopened the research.

Downstream cost did not collapse. If an engineer or an AI coding agent builds from the spec, a fabricated premise becomes working code within hours. You find out the premise was invented after the feature ships, not before.

Review habits didn't adapt. Teams review specs for clarity, completeness and tone, which are the properties language models are best at producing. Almost nobody reviews for provenance. Developers have already learned this lesson with code: 66% name "AI solutions that are almost right, but not quite" as their biggest frustration, and 45% say debugging AI-generated code takes longer (Stack Overflow, 2025). An almost-right spec is worse, because nothing fails to compile.

Professional documents are not immune. In October 2025, Deloitte agreed to repay the final instalment of an AU$440,000 report for an Australian government department after a researcher found fabricated academic references and a made-up quote from a court judgment. The corrected version removed more than a dozen bogus references and footnotes and disclosed that a GPT-4o based tool chain had been used (The Register, 2025). A spec rarely gets that much outside scrutiny, which is why the check has to happen inside the team.

The fix is not "don't use AI to draft". It is to add the review step the draft skipped: trace every claim to a source.

Read the spec once, sentence by sentence. Every sentence that asserts something about users, the market, the product or the future gets exactly one of four labels.

"Three of the last five onboarding calls mention exporting to spreadsheets" is sourced if you can link those three calls. The test is mechanical: a teammate who wasn't in the room can open the link and see the same thing.

"Admins likely export because they report upward" is inferred from those calls. That is fine, as long as it is labelled and the underlying sources are attached. Inference is where product judgement lives; the problem is only unlabelled inference.

Assumptions are not shameful. Specs run on them. The failure is when an assumption is written in the same voice as a finding, so the reader absorbs it instead of questioning it.

This is the label that matters most. Invented claims are the model's contribution to the document, and they are the ones to delete or convert into questions.

Do not argue about edge cases between labels. If you hesitate between Inferred and Assumed, pick Assumed. The label is a prompt to look closer, not a verdict.

A simple decision rule for the end of step 4: if more than a third of the claims in the Problem section came back Invented, send the spec back to discovery rather than to engineering.

Take a made-up but typical AI draft for an admin export feature. The Problem section says:

Admins export data manually every week, spending hours in spreadsheets. Reporting is the top request among churned accounts. A native export will reduce churn.

Four claims: two deleted, two demoted, zero sourced. The rewritten Problem section is shorter and honest:

One onboarding call (linked) mentioned exporting account data to a spreadsheet. We don't yet know how often admins export or why. Open question: what did churned accounts say when they left? Hypothesis: a native export reduces churn among accounts with more than 5 admins. We will check this before committing to a build.

That document can still lead to a build. But the team now knows what it is betting on, and the person reading it can disagree with the assumption instead of absorbing it.

The ledger is the list of every Assumed claim, and it is your discovery plan. Record four fields per row:

A spec with a short, owned ledger can move fast safely. A spec with no ledger either has no assumptions, which is almost never true, or has hidden them. For a fuller method of ranking assumptions by importance and evidence, see our guide to assumption mapping, or sort them on the free assumption map.

A manual trace works, but it relies on discipline at the exact moment discipline is cheapest to skip. It is better to ask for provenance while the draft is produced. Anthropic's documentation lists the core techniques (Anthropic):

The Kalai paper suggests a further idea: state a confidence target in the prompt. For example, answer only if you are more than 75% confident, because a wrong answer costs 2 points and "I don't know" costs nothing (Kalai et al., 2025). Applied to specs, that means rewarding the model for writing "no source" instead of filling the gap fluently.

Here is a prompt block you can paste under any spec request:

Anthropic is clear that these techniques reduce hallucinations but do not eliminate them. The prompt makes the Claim Trace faster; it does not replace it. If you are drafting the whole PRD with AI, our AI PRD guide covers the rest of the workflow.

The method tells you whether a claim has a source. It does not tell you whether the source is good. A fully sourced spec can still describe the wrong thing, because sources can be biased, thin or unrepresentative. Pair the trace with a check on evidence quality: how many independent sources, how recent, and how close to a real buying or usage decision. Our post on what counts as evidence for each product risk gives a quick scale for this.

It also covers only part of the risk. Marty Cagan's four big risks are value, usability, feasibility and business viability, and he puts value and viability on the product manager (SVPG, 2017). AI has made a working prototype cheap, which shrinks some feasibility questions. It has not made value or viability any easier to know, and those are exactly the sections where invented claims cluster.

Finally, the audit does not replace judgement about what to build. It makes judgement visible. Once a spec has passed, hand it over in a format engineers will use: our guide to the technical spec engineers actually read covers that step, and the acceptance checklist for AI-generated code covers reviewing what comes back.

The Claim Trace is easiest when provenance is captured at write time. That is the design stance behind Specky. Interviews, tickets, feedback and decisions live as connected nodes in a Product Graph, and when the AI drafts a spec or insight it cites the specific evidence it used, inline. Drafts land in the PM Inbox for human review rather than going straight into the record, so a reviewer can see which sentences carry citations and which carry none. You can approximate the same thing in any tool with the prompt above; the point is that "no source" should be visible, not hidden.

It is when an AI-written PRD or spec states something about users, the market or expected results that no source supports, written in the same confident voice as the sourced parts. It usually appears as an invented reason (a usage pattern, a ranking, a churn driver) rather than an invented feature, which is why reviewers miss it.

About 15 minutes for a typical one-to-three-page spec: 3 minutes to extract claims, 5 to trace them, 4 to act on each label and 3 to re-read. Give yourself 60 seconds per claim to find a source. Longer specs scale roughly with the number of factual claims, not the page count.

Partly. Asking the model to find a supporting quote for each claim and retract any it can't support is one of Anthropic's recommended techniques, and it catches many extrinsic hallucinations. It is not enough on its own, because the model can misread a source or quote something that doesn't actually support the claim. A human still checks the links.

An assumption is a belief the team holds and knows it hasn't checked. A hallucination is a claim with no origin that nobody on the team actually believed until the model wrote it. Labelled assumptions are useful because they become tests. Hallucinations should be deleted or turned into questions.

No. AI drafting saves real time on structure, edge cases and wording. The risk sits in the evidence sections. Supply the research, tickets and data as inputs, ask the model to mark sources and assumptions, and run a Claim Trace before the spec goes to engineering or to a coding agent.

Stop the build and go back to discovery. If more than a third of the problem claims are invented, the feature doesn't yet have a demonstrated problem. Turn each invented claim into a question, pick the two that would most change the decision, and test those first with interviews, a usage query or a support-ticket review.

How to use Ollama and LM Studio models as real agents with tools on a Mac

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Pointing a chat UI at an Ollama or LM Studio model is easy. Making that model behave as an agent that edits your files and runs commands is where it gets messy, because open-weight models and different OpenAI-compatible servers are inconsistent about tool calling.

Short answer: JoeBro is a free, open-source Mac workspace for your AI agents. Point it at Ollama, LM Studio or any OpenAI-compatible server and, in agent mode, local models get the same tools as hosted ones (files, web, MCP servers, plugins) and the same shared memory. Local models (Ollama, LM Studio, anything OpenAI-compatible) and cloud models with your own keys run as agents with tools. This is a list of the specific failures I hit and what the Python backend (standard library only) does about each one.

The OpenAI tool-call format says arguments is a JSON string. In practice some endpoints hand back an already-parsed object, and some models wrap the JSON in a markdown code fence. The parser accepts a dict as-is, strips a leading fence if there is one, tries json.loads, and never raises. The worst case is an empty dict.

When a reply hits max_tokens halfway through a tool call (typically a model rewriting a long file in one content argument), the arguments are non-empty but not valid JSON. If you parse that to {}, the tool fails with a misleading "requires path". So there is a separate check: arguments that are non-empty and don't parse mean "truncated", and the model gets told that, rather than a bogus missing-parameter error.

Models invent parameter names. A document tool wants path, and one model sends file, another filepath, another file_name. Rather than dropping them, there is a small alias table: file, file_path, filename and friends all map to path; old_string and search map to find; new_text and replacement map to replace. It's unglamorous and it removes a whole class of failed turns.

Some models don't use the native tool-call channel at all and write the call into the reply as markup: , , , or DeepSeek's DSML wrappers. A normaliser rewrites each of those into one internal form before execution. If tools were run from text after the model had already finished its turn, one extra completion is made with tools switched off: "Here are the results of the tool calls you just made, give your final answer now".

A single cat of a big file can fill a small local context. Each tool reply is clipped to 24,000 characters, keeping 70% from the head and 30% from the tail, with a note in the middle saying how much was cut and to narrow the request. The head of a file shows its shape and the tail of a log has the error; the middle is usually filler.

For some tasks I want thinking off. The switch isn't the same everywhere: OpenAI wants a minimal reasoning effort, Anthropic's extended thinking is opt-in, several hosted providers accept reasoning_effort: none, and Ollama reads a chat-template flag instead (chat_template_kwargs: {enable_thinking: false}). The request builder branches on the endpoint.

Agents that run commands get a permission step. When the app has "ask before running" on, a command waits for an explicit approve or deny (default deny, and a 3 minute timeout), and you can allow a tool for the rest of the session. Document edits can be gated the same way, before the file is touched.

Because these agents sit in the same workspace as Claude Code, Codex and OpenCode, they share the same per-project memory over MCP and the same pool of tools, MCP servers and plugins. A local model can pick up a fact that Claude Code learned yesterday.

None of this is clever. It's the pile of small compatibility fixes you need before a local model is usable as an agent, and I'd be glad to hear which quirks you've hit that I haven't.

JoeBro is GPLv3 and free, and local first: chats and memory live in a SQLite file, with no account and no telemetry. One-line install (macOS 26+):

Code: https://github.com/joexk1/JoeBro . Short answers to common questions: https://joebro.pages.dev/faq/. A star helps other people find it.

Node.js Signed Uploads for Expiring Support Documents (with Private Object Storage)

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A signed customer-support document can be available for 30 days and still need to disappear at a precise deadline. That requirement changes the design. I would keep the object private, give each document a unique key, store its deletion deadline in Postgres, and issue short-lived signed URLs for transfer. The Node.js application remains the authority on who may read the file and when deletion is due.

TL;DR: use a signed browser upload only when the bucket's existing CORS policy already accepts your origin. Otherwise, proxy the bytes through Node.js. In both cases, treat URL expiry and document retention as separate clocks. The app owns the exact deletion deadline; a storage lifecycle rule, where available, is only a daily backstop.

This is the distinction that matters for a one-person SaaS. Moving bytes is undifferentiated work. Making a deletion promise, and being able to show how it is enforced, belongs in the product.

Infrai fits one narrow part of this workflow: issuing private signed transfer authorization when the bucket's existing CORS policy already suits the browser. The application still owns access decisions, retention deadlines, deletion work, and evidence.

No. It expires permission to use one URL. It does not delete the object, invalidate every other URL already issued, or prove that a processor removed the bytes.

I model a retained document as an application record first and a storage object second. A useful record contains a random document ID, the support case ID, an object key, deleteAfter, a generation number, and a state such as pending, available, deleting, or deleted. The browser never chooses the final key. A key like support-cases/{caseId}/signed/{documentId}.pdf makes ownership legible while avoiding an overwrite-prone shared filename.

This storage surface has a minimum lifecycle granularity of one day. That cannot express an hourly contractual deadline. Run deletion from the application deadline, then reconcile overdue database rows; use lifecycle cleanup to catch drift rather than define the promise.

This also explains why I would not reuse users/{userId}/avatar.jpg for this workload. Overwrite-in-place can be acceptable for an avatar with a clear last-write-wins rule. A signed support document needs a stable identity. The storage surface has no object versioning, object lock, or If-Match conditional write, so an accidental overwrite is not recoverable and the object layer cannot provide strict write exclusion. A unique key plus a database generation closes the race that the object API does not.

The smallest design I would ship has two database transactions around storage work. Creation first inserts a pending row with its deadline and unique key. Only then does the application authorize a transfer. Deletion later claims a due row by generation, removes the object, and marks the row deleted. A reconciliation worker repeatedly finds due rows that have not reached that terminal state.

The following TypeScript is intentionally about the part the application must own. It also reads the live discovery contract before an adapter relies on the presign route. The discovery response supplies the request schema and runnable examples, so the adapter does not need a copied, possibly stale request body from an article.

The public discovery request needs no key. The authenticated presign request described by that contract must use Authorization: Bearer ${process.env.INFRAI_API_KEY} and an explicit HTTP method. Never send that authorization header to the returned presigned URL; the browser uses the signed URL as returned. Check every response, and on HTTP 429 honor Retry-After or back off exponentially. Any retried write should use an idempotency key so a retry cannot apply twice.

Before issuing a signed read, the app checks the caller's case access, the row state, and deleteAfter. This is important during deletion: once a worker claims the row, new reads stop even if physical deletion has not finished. After deletion succeeds, the worker records deleted. If it stops between those operations, reconciliation resumes the same transition.

A direct browser upload keeps file bytes out of the Node.js process. The app authenticates the customer, allocates the key, and authorizes a narrowly scoped transfer. That is my default when the browser origin, method, and headers already match the bucket's CORS policy.

There is a real boundary here. Custom bucket CORS is not self-service on Infrai's storage surface. If the existing policy does not fit, a direct browser transfer is not something to force with frontend tricks. Proxy the upload through Node.js, accept the bandwidth and backpressure cost, and keep the browser talking only to your own origin. A proxy is also the clearer choice when every byte must pass through synchronous inspection before storage.

Files stay private either way. This storage does not support public or public-read ACLs, and public_url remains null. That makes it unsuitable for permanent public avatar URLs, an image host, or static-site delivery. It does fit confidential support files served through short-lived signed reads.

The platform is worth considering for the presign boundary because its API is self-describing: public discovery returns the request schema, response schema, billing information, and runnable examples for the capability. Every documented capability has examples in 10 languages.

Infrai provides 295 routes across 20 modules under one key. Its operating model is one key, one wallet, and one bill. For this workflow, the same API key covers the storage capability instead of adding a separate vendor credential, and unified billing removes another invoice from month-end reconciliation. That is small, recurring operational work I would rather trade for a weekly product release. No provider-specific SDK is added only to authorize a transfer.

I recommend trying Infrai for private signed transfer authorization when an existing bucket CORS policy fits, because discovery makes the adapter contract inspectable before implementation and keeps this small integration replaceable. Keep consent, authorization, deadline calculation, deletion scheduling, and evidence in the application. Those do not become storage responsibilities.

The facade is not the processor contract. Region, replication, erasure terms, and legal commitments still have to be checked against the selected specialist provider. It covers R2, S3, OSS, and COS for storage, but not GCS or B2. It offers no automatic cross-region replication or cross-cloud bulk migration tool.

That produces a more useful comparison than a price table:

AWS S3's lifecycle documentation is useful for understanding provider-managed expiry, but lifecycle configuration alone should not be confused with the application's exact deadline. R2, OSS, and COS likewise deserve direct evaluation when their native region or contract is the reason for the choice. A wrapper cannot create a residency or deletion guarantee absent from the underlying agreement.

For records requiring WORM retention, recoverable versions, or financial-grade immutability, use a specialist or direct provider that contractually supplies those controls. Infrai's lack of object lock and versioning is decisive there. No naming convention fixes it.

The first scale upgrade is a durable deletion queue plus a periodic database scan for overdue rows. The queue handles normal timing. The scan detects missed work. I would alert on the age and count of overdue rows, not on whether a job was merely dispatched.

Next, I would make processor and region part of each document's recorded placement decision. That gives migration work an inventory. Since object metadata cannot be searched server-side and listing supports prefix filtering only, Postgres should remain the operational index.

Large multipart uploads need their own cleanup path because abandoned fragments have no automatic cleanup rule here. Retention policy must cover those fragments too, even though they never became an available document.

Finally, I would test races deliberately. Two updates cannot rely on If-Match, because it is unavailable. The database generation decides which operation is current; a stale worker must fail before it can remove a newer object. Last-write-wins is fine only when it is an explicit product rule.

My shipping rule is compact: the application owns truth and time; private object storage holds bytes; signed URLs grant brief transfer authority. Use a direct specialist when native retention, immutability, CORS, residency, or replication controls are part of the promise. Outsource the plumbing only where the boundary stays honest.

If that boundary fits your system, start with the Infrai guide to signed avatar uploads and verify the live discovery schema before writing the adapter.

AI just exposed a major weakness in anonymous peer reviews

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The model saw one report at a time across 398 reviews and meta-reviews. It scored lower on a larger set of public reviews.

Anthropic’s Claude Fable 5.2 correctly named the writer of 56% of 398 reviews and meta-reviews in a small test of unsigned peer reviews. Six volunteers wrote the reports. The model saw one report at a time, without web access, tools, a list of possible authors or other writing samples.

In a Nov. 19 preprint, Atrey Desai and colleagues analyzed reports written between 2015 and 2026 for 26 double-blind conferences and workshops in machine learning and natural language processing. The researchers knew who wrote each report, but the reviewers’ names had never been publicly linked to them. In 19 cases, the text itself was already publicly available without a name.

Claude named the writer on 68.4% of 266 standard reviews and 31.1% of 132 meta-reviews, which summarize the reviews of a submission. The meta-reviews were shorter and came from just two of the six contributors. The study did not separate the effects of report length, type and authorship.

The 56% result counts each report equally, so contributors who supplied more reports had more influence on the overall figure. When the researchers gave each reviewer equal weight, Claude’s average accuracy was 49.1%. The test showed that an unsigned report can give its writer away. Estimating how often that happens across confidential peer review would take a larger, more varied sample.

Claude Fable 5.2 received one text per attempt, without web access, tools, candidate names or additional writing samples. Both bars use a common 0% to 100% scale. The rates are reported in Table 32.

The meta-reviews were shorter. The study cannot separate the effects of report length, type and writer.

The researchers also tested 1,559 public reviews from 47 open-review journals. Claude correctly identified the reviewer in 21.4% of the 1,530 cases it answered. The paper reports 39.3% accuracy for a subset containing 789 reviews that Pangram 4.1, an AI-text detector, had labeled human or mixed. The paper’s abstract cites that filtered rate, rounded to 39%.

Accuracy was higher on public reviews whose writers cited their own work. Claude recorded 80.2% accuracy in the group of 106 reviews with self-citations and 17.1% in the group of 1,453 without them.

Claude made mistakes even in the group where it performed best. An editor or author presented with a name from Claude would still need independent evidence to establish who wrote an unsigned review. CryptoSlate has also reported on how frontier AI models handled fabricated research data. The team says it plans to add results from a substantially larger set of anonymous peer reviews in an update to the preprint.

Guarding Stars Was Adorable, But Can We Talk About That Stalker Twist?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Internet Can’t Stop Talking About People’s Mouths In Netflix’s The Christmas Switch. I Can’t Unsee It

I'm Loving Netflix's Win The Mall (And Haley Baylee), But There's One Thing I'd Like To See More Of

The Cliff Booth Reviews Point Out A Missed Opportunity In Brad Pitt’s Sequel, And I Have To Agree

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

There was some soggy fruitcake amidst this Christmasy feast.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

This year’s Christmas movie releases are in full swing now that it’s mid-November, and I knew I had to check out Guarding Stars with my Netflix subscription. I mean, it unites Gilmore Girls’ Jared Padalecki with Gossip Girl’s Leighton Meester, so I was in for the 2000s nostalgia alone. I was really digging this lighthearted rom-com (that’s sometimes an action movie), but then that twist just made me go “WTF!” Let’s talk about it. But be warned, there are SPOILERS ahead!

Before we get into my lows about Guarding Stars, I have to give the movie its flowers. I’ve seen a lot of holiday rom-coms, and this one had the right amount of sweetness and depth I want from this kind of movie. It follows the fake dating trope pretty much to a T, but I really enjoyed what Padalecki and Meester in particular brought to it as Jack and Hannah.

It’s the kind of movie where we all know they were going to fall in love by the end, but I was instantly curious about how these two were going to fall for each other. I like that they brought out the softness in each other despite both of them being otherwise hardened by their current lives, and I really liked the mother/son dynamic between Jack and Andie Macdowell’s Connie.

Guarding Stars also had that nice balance of goofiness, wit and emotion that I want to see from a Christmas Netflix movie that stopped it from falling into simply being a holiday screensaver. I know it’s based on a popular book called The Bodyguard, and after seeing it I would definitely pick it up, especially to see what changes were made.

However, I thought the Guarding Stars really dropped the ball on the whole “stalker” plotline. Not that I’d expect it to be a huge part of the movie to begin with, but when it was revealed that Nadine was Jack’s stalker near the end, I was so taken aback. It felt way too convenient that one of two people he meets outside of his family and his bodyguard crew ended up being the stalker, and particularly having happened to be in the same place where he was hiding away.

To me, it would have made a lot more sense if the stalker travelled to his location after seeing the kiss cam video with Hannah go viral. Instead, the only information we get is that they knew each other in high school and she divorced her husband to be with him. And then, her husband was somehow vengeful of Jack? The stalker payoff was so unbelievably thin, and felt like a bummer. Especially when the rest of the rom-com put a lot of attention on character development and fun moments for the other characters.

Perhaps the filmmaking team thought the audience wouldn’t care about the plotline not concerning the romance? But, to me, it just cheapened the rest of the viewing experience. I hope more Netflix rom-coms can build out their scripts. If it’s going to be partly an action mystery, I don’t want to feel like no attention was put on that aspect of the movie.

Look, all in all, Guarding Stars had some elements that had me putting it on the nice and naughty list, but overall, I’m happy I checked it out. But come on, Netflix, if you want to shock me, I know you can do better than that.

Windows 7 gets a second wind with modern GPU support.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Windows 7 may have been retired years ago, but that hasn’t stopped enthusiasts from finding ways to bring modern hardware and features to Microsoft’s beloved operating system. A new community project is now giving Windows 7 the ability to support modern Nvidia, AMD, and Intel graphics cards, along with DirectX 12 and Vulkan 1.4 capabilities.

Developed by enthusiasts EAZY BLACK, kryptik, and VisualEhrmanntraut, the project backports WDDM 2.2 from Windows 10. Interestingly, Windows 7 originally launched with WDDM 1.1, while Microsoft introduced WDDM 2.0 with Windows 10 in 2015 and WDDM 2.2 with the Creators Update in 2017. Microsoft also introduced a limited version of DirectX 12 for Windows 7 in 2019; however, that implementation was only meant for developers to port select games to the older operating system.

According to a post by @TheBobPony on X, Nvidia's RTX 40 and RTX 50 series, AMD's Radeon RX 7000 and RX 9000 series, and select Intel Arc graphics cards are supported. Screenshots confirm that the backport enables GPUs to be recognized by Windows 7 as well as third-party apps like GPU-Z. It is also claimed that features such as ray tracing and DLSS also work on compatible hardware. Older GPUs, including Nvidia's RTX 30 series and AMD's Radeon RX 6000 series, may also benefit from the project. That said, actual feature support depends on the graphics card, driver implementation, and application.

For anyone who still prefers Windows 7 or relies on older software and hardware, this project is worth exploring, especially if you intend to extend the usefulness of the operating system. Users can also combine it with the VxKex compatibility layer, allowing newer Windows apps to run on older operating systems. You can grab the project files and modified drivers by heading to the W7Revived website.

There’s a reason why enthusiasts continue to keep Windows 7 alive. Released in 2009 as a successor to Windows Vista, it offered a familiar interface, solid performance, and a straightforward desktop experience. It became one of Microsoft’s most popular operating systems and remained a favorite among PC enthusiasts and everyday users long after the arrival of Windows 10.

In fact, Windows 7 still has some life left in it. We recently tested the aging operating system against Windows 11 on a modern PC running on an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D, Radeon RX 6950 XT, and 64GB of DDR5 memory. The tests included eight DirectX 11 and two DirectX 9 titles, with Windows 7 delivering better performance in certain scenarios, particularly CPU-bound games, along with stronger 1% lows in some titles.

Boeing's Most Modern Engines Are Quieter, But Its Tricks Weren't Copied By Other Planemakers For One Main Reason

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One of the most distinctive features of the Boeing 787 Dreamliner is the unusual sawtooth pattern at the back of the engine nacelle. They're called 'chevrons', and their primary purpose is to reduce the aircraft's noise levels. The 787 wasn't only intended to be game-changing for airlines; it was also meant to be a major leap forward in noise reduction and the passenger experience. Boeing also incorporated chevrons into the engine nacelles of the 747-8 (which came out right before the 787) and the 737 MAX.

Boeing patented the chevron design that it implemented into these three aircraft, which is why other manufacturers have not incorporated this exact design. However, the patents for Boeing's chevrons have since expired, and Airbus has publicly rejected integrating chevrons into its contemporary aircraft during this period. Even more interestingly, Boeing has decided not to integrate chevrons into the upcoming 777X, reversing course after initially planning them during early development.

The main reason Boeing designed chevrons for the 787, 747-8, and 737 MAX is noise reduction. They smooth the mixing of hot exhaust air coming out of the engine core with the cooler bypass air flowing around the engine. By blending the air more smoothly, the engine produces less aerodynamic noise, allowing Boeing's newest airliners to more easily comply with increasingly strict noise regulations while providing a more comfortable experience for passengers.

Multiple aircraft types also feature chevrons, but other types incorporate them in the inner nozzle, and they are part of the engine rather than a design choice by the airframe. The 787, 747-8, and 737 MAX are the only types widely used today that feature chevrons on the nacelle (which are not actually a part of the engine itself), and they're one of the most distinctive features on modern Boeing airliners. In addition, the company also originally depicted the upcoming 777X with chevrons in concept renderings.

However, Airbus notably rejected the idea of using chevrons on the A320neo, A350, and A330neo, all of which entered service in the 2010s. In addition, the 777X will not feature chevrons, and there's no indication that either Boeing or Airbus plans to employ them in future aircraft projects. While the chevrons were an innovative new idea in the 2000s and early 2010s, when the 787, 747-8, and 737 MAX were being developed, they have largely been rendered obsolete today.

The vortices generated by the chevrons take energy away from the airflow coming out of the rear of the engine. Because they protrude into the airflow, they also increase drag. As such, it's estimated that chevrons may reduce thrust by up to 0.5% and lead to a slight increase in fuel burn. This is a small amount, but aircraft engineering and design is all about trade-offs to achieve an airframe's desired goals. For Boeing, this was initially worth it to reduce noise levels.

Airbus, however, did not view the slight noise reduction as worth the trade-off in fuel efficiency. Instead, its latest aircraft, like the A350, focus more heavily on acoustic treatments within the nacelle, such as sound-absorbing lining, to reduce noise emissions. Both are valid approaches, and they show how Airbus and Boeing pursue different aircraft designs to achieve largely the same goals: cost-effective, competitive airliners that the market wants. Both companies have made very different choices and have found success doing so.

(737 MAX 7, 737 MAX 8, 737 MAX 9, 737 MAX 10)

While the chevrons do slightly reduce effective thrust and add drag, they can allow Boeing to slightly reduce heavy sound-deadening in the fuselage. This reduces weight, which improves fuel efficiency. As with all aircraft engineering decisions, there are benefits and drawbacks to the chevrons. Boeing decided that the benefits outweighed the negatives and patented its design. Airbus opted to pursue a different direction rather than develop its own chevron design.

The battle between two aviation giants heats up as they strive for the quietest cabin experience.

The 777X does not have chevrons on its engine nacelles, a change made early in the aircraft's development after initial renderings were released with chevrons. Ultimately, operating costs are extremely important to airlines, and the chevrons' direct fuel-burn impact, while minor, is not ideal. The 777X, however, is Boeing's newest aircraft program, which means that it has years of experience from when the 787 first began development, designing technology to minimize the noise impact of its planes.

While Boeing's new approach to keeping noise levels low for the 777X has not been fully disclosed, the most likely explanation is more advanced acoustic treatments inside the engine nacelle, similar to what Airbus has implemented with the A350. Innovations likely make these solutions more effective at reducing noise while potentially weighing less. In turn, this means that Boeing no longer needs to implement chevrons, which work well at eliminating noise but slightly hurt fuel efficiency.

In addition to sound acoustics, the 777X's GE9X engines are truly enormous, with a fan diameter of 134 inches (340 centimeters) and a bypass ratio of 10:1. High bypass engines like these generate significantly less noise than turbofans with lower bypass ratios, as less air proportionally is being fed through the engine core. Until further advancements in chevron design reduce the fuel efficiency impact, it's unlikely that future airlines will incorporate them to reduce noise.

Airbus and Boeing design aircraft that achieve very similar results for airlines, but take very different approaches to get there. There are several main examples, such as how the fuselage is assembled on the 787 and A350. The Dreamliner's fuselage is made up of several 'barrels', which reduces weight and improves fuel efficiency. The A350, however, uses traditional panel construction, which is easier to fabricate and comes with lower maintenance costs. Both are valid approaches.

Airbus has tended to favor smaller passenger windows on its aircraft compared to contemporary Boeing aircraft. Smaller windows save weight, but Boeing bets that larger windows deliver a better passenger experience. Boeing designed the 787 and 777X with raked wingtips, which have an aerodynamic advantage on long-haul routes. The A350 uses blended winglets, which effectively increase the aircraft's wingspan while still allowing it to fit within standard airport gates.

In addition to these minor design changes, you also see differences in how they approach various aircraft projects. The 777X, for example, is a significant overhaul of the legacy 777 airframe, featuring a fuselage stretch, a brand-new carbon-composite wing, new cabin windows, a new interior, new engines, and a new cockpit. The A330neo, also an overhaul of a 1990s-era widebody, only features a new engine, a new winglet design, and a new interior, along with software enhancements.

In the 1960s, a Boeing 707 taking off could easily exceed 110 decibels. Today, a 787 taking off typically produces noise that is under 95 decibels. The decibel scale is logarithmic, meaning the 707 was more than four times as loud as the 787, even though the 787 has a maximum takeoff weight (MTOW) that can be nearly double that of the 707 and engines that are significantly more powerful. Admittedly, the 787 has only two engines instead of four, but it still represents a significant achievement in reducing aircraft noise levels.

Over the years, ICAO has set noise standards for aircraft, with deadlines for when aircraft are expected to comply with the new limitations. Other regulators, such as the FAA, may set their own standards that roughly align with ICAO's. FAA Stage Five noise standards require a cumulative reduction of seven decibels compared to Stage Four standards, which equals a cumulative reduction of 17 decibels compared to the older Stage Three standards.

The FAA's Stage Five standards, which took effect in 2017, only applied to new aircraft types submitted for review after the standards were implemented, and it did not require already-certified types to be in compliance (however, newer types like the 787 were already in compliance anyway). In the past, the FAA did require pre-existing types to meet newer standards, most notably when Stage Three standards took effect. This required manufacturers to develop 'hush kits' for types like the 727 to lower their noise emissions.

Beyond exterior noise, passengers have benefited from noise reductions, since a loud flight can be extremely fatiguing. More advanced acoustic nacelle treatments and innovations such as chevrons have allowed manufacturers to reduce heavy sound-deadening while still providing an overall quieter experience. As innovations in aircraft design continue, airliners will get quieter both outside and inside the cabin, while also becoming even more fuel-efficient.

The advantages of using a USB-C docking station

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Plus, there are differences between a USB-C hub and a docking station.

Laptops have been getting thinner and lighter from year to year, and they have become so powerful that many people choose a laptop as their main computer. But a thin body means fewer ports, and a desktop setup with a screen, mouse, webcam and keyboard requires a lot of them. If you have a laptop and still want the full desktop experience, a USB-C docking station can be the solution you're looking for.

A USB-C docking station is a box that connects to your laptop through a single USB-C cable and gives it a full set of ports. USB-C docking stations reduce cord clutter, add more ports to your setup and can keep your laptop's own ports from wearing down.

The biggest advantage of a dock is simplicity. One single cable connects the laptop to its peripherals. That means you can come home and plug in all your devices with one plug; you can also grab your laptop on your way out just as quickly. Monitors, a keyboard, a webcam and a charger no longer need to be reconnected every time the laptop comes and goes, and your desk stays tidier because the cables run to the dock instead of the laptop.

A dock can also add ports your laptop doesn't have. Depending on the model, you can get HDMI or DisplayPort outputs for external monitors, more USB ports, an SD card reader and an Ethernet port for a stable wired internet connection. A thin laptop that comes with three or four ports can end up with a dozen.

The third advantage is less wear on your laptop. Plugging and unplugging several cables over and over puts strain on the ports, and a dock takes that job over. Instead of plugging several cables into the laptop every day, you plug in just one.

If you only need a few basic connections, a USB-C hub is the cheaper option. Hubs have roughly four to 10 ports and cost anywhere between $30 and $150. Docking stations generally have more ports, come with their own DC power adapter, and cost more, with some higher-end models costing more than $400. A dock can give you a full desktop setup with multiple monitors, plus more ports of every kind, and the higher-end Thunderbolt 4 and USB4 models can run multiple displays, Ethernet, audio and laptop charging through that one cable. If that sounds like what you need, here are the best docking stations for laptops.

Before buying, it's worth checking a few things, because not every dock works with every laptop. A USB-C port only outputs video if it supports DisplayPort Alt Mode or is a Thunderbolt or USB4 port, and the dock needs to support Power Delivery pass-through at your laptop's required wattage. If you plan to work with the lid closed, keep the power plugged in, since the laptop draws more power with an external monitor attached.

Get those details right and a dock quietly does its job, providing a laptop that goes back to being portable by unplugging just one cable.

He’s not expecting them to suddenly forget all their differences, but it boggles the mind that they haven’t come out with any sort of supportive statement. Even a few kind words would mean so much, but instead, there’s this deafening silence.

The comments above should be taken with a massive grain of salt, and they essentially amount to rumors at this time. Other reports continue to swirl around Prince Harry and Meghan Markle during this time as well. For instance, some insiders have alleged that Markle’s businesses have seen decreases in sales due to the allegations against her father. The drama aside, though, the Duke and Duchess have reportedly been making holiday plans, and Harry confirmed that despite their move, they’d spend it stateside.

Meanwhile, Thomas Markle has been ruled mentally competent to stand trial for his reported offense. A preliminary hearing is set to occur in Tampa on December 1, and an actual trial would take place in California. When it comes to whether the British Royal Family – which recently commented on the 007 casting – will weigh in on Markle’s case amid the legal proceedings, that remains to be seen.

Nike vs. Procter & Gamble: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nike's direct-to-consumer push meets Procter & Gamble's everyday essentials, but their valuations and risk profiles tell very different stories for 2027.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 22, 2026 at 2:51PM EST

Choosing between Nike (NKE -4.11%) and Procter & Gamble (PG +0.91%) involves weighing the high-performance growth potential of athletic apparel against the defensive stability of household essentials as investors look toward 2027.

Nike dominates the footwear market by blending product innovation with powerful brand marketing, while Procter & Gamble operates a vast empire of daily necessities like soaps and detergents. Both companies offer immense global reach, but they serve different roles in a diversified portfolio, making it vital to understand their current financial health.

Nike designs and sells athletic footwear and apparel globally, focusing on its Nike Direct business and a vast network of wholesale accounts. The company utilizes a mix of retail stores and digital platforms to reach consumers in over 40 countries. In the United States, customer concentration is notable as the three largest customers account for roughly 29% of sales, which adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Aug. 31, 2026, revenue reached nearly $45.9 billion, which was a decrease of approximately 1.2% from the previous twelve-month period. Despite this slight decline in sales, the company generated a net income of roughly $3.1 billion. This performance resulted in a net margin of close to 6.7% for the period.

As of its May 2026 balance sheet, the company maintained a debt-to-equity ratio of approximately 0.7x. Debt-to-equity is a measure of total debt relative to shareholder equity, indicating how much the company relies on borrowing to fund its operations. The current ratio is roughly 2.0x, which measures a company's ability to cover its short-term liabilities with short-term assets. In the fiscal year ended May 31, 2026, free cash flow was approximately $2.2 billion. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 24.9% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Procter & Gamble manages a diverse portfolio of essential brands, operating as a leader among consumer staples stocks across categories like healthcare and family care. The company sells these daily necessities through various channels, including grocery stores, pharmacies, and mass merchandisers. A significant portion of its business is concentrated with Walmart (WMT +1.51%), which accounts for approximately 16% of total sales.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $87.9 billion, representing a growth rate of roughly 3.5% compared to the prior period. This consistent performance resulted in a net income of nearly $16.0 billion for the year. High net margin levels of roughly 18.2% highlight the company's ability to maintain profitability while navigating fluctuating costs.

As of its June 2026 balance sheet, the company carries a debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 0.6x. Debt-to-equity is a measure of total debt relative to shareholder equity, and this level suggests a moderate use of borrowing. The current ratio, which measures a company's ability to cover short-term debts with assets it can quickly convert to cash, is approximately 0.7x. In the fiscal year ended June 30, 2026, free cash flow was close to $15.1 billion.

Nike faces intense global competition from various athletic and lifestyle footwear and apparel companies such as adidas (ADDDF -6.04%) and Under Armour (UAA -1.56%). The company relies heavily on a concentrated base of contract manufacturers and suppliers located outside the United States, which creates vulnerability to supply chain disruptions. Additionally, volatility in global economic conditions and a significant reliance on complex information technology systems pose ongoing operational risks.

Procter & Gamble contends with significant competition across diverse product categories from both global competitors and private-label brands. The company is dependent on complex global supply chains and faces constant pressure from fluctuations in the costs of commodities, energy, and transportation. Furthermore, managing compliance with a wide array of global legal, regulatory, and tax requirements remains a primary challenge for the organization.

Procter & Gamble currently offers a lower valuation based on future earnings estimates, while Nike provides a lower entry point based on its P/S ratio.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with Procter & Gamble. It owns the everyday brands like Tide, Pampers, and Gillette that people buy again and again, and it turns that loyalty into steady cash for shareholders. P&G has raised its dividend for an incredible 70 straight years and has paid one for well over a century. What really stands out to me right now is how broad the growth is. Sales rose 4% in its latest quarter, above what the company had guided for the year, and all five of its business segments grew.

To give Nike its due, its running and other sports businesses are growing, and it has a CEO working to win back customers. But there's no way around it: Nike's business is in a tough spot right now. It expects sales to fall this fiscal year as it cuts back some lifestyle shoes and overhauls how it sells in China

P&G, by contrast, is growing from a very steady base. For long-term investors, that mix of trusted brands, steady growth, and a rising dividend makes P&G the better buy for 2027.

Uber Trades at 16 Times Forward Earnings and a 30% Discount to Its All-Time High Stock Price. Here’s Why Robotaxis Make That a Mistake.

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sentiment toward this disruptive business reflects the market's fears about autonomous vehicle technology.

By Neil Patel – Nov 22, 2026 at 2:45PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Uber Technologies ›

When looking at a list of the most innovative and disruptive businesses this century, the investment community will agree that Uber Technologies (UBER -3.25%) is impossible to ignore. It completely upended the taxi industry, leveraging the prevalence of smartphones, the app economy, and faster connectivity to become a global technology giant.

The impressive position Uber has built, however, hasn't prevented the growth stock from lagging the broader market in the past five years. And it now trades at what appears to be a very compelling forward price-to-earnings ratio of 16.4, even though its financial performance is strong.

Management might be feeling the weight of the market's worries about the potential threat that autonomous vehicles (AV) will pose for the company's operations. There's a valid argument to be made that this exciting new technology could end up being a major opportunity for Uber, though. And that alters the investment thesis, signalling that shares are attractively priced right now.

When investors hear about developments within the AV industry, two companies come to mind. The market leader is Alphabet's (GOOGL -2.70%) (GOOG -2.59%) Waymo. It's backed by one of the most dominant technology companies in the world, which means it has top-tier talent and software/hardware capabilities that are hard to beat.

Waymo is winning the race. The platform currently operates in 15 U.S. cities. There are plans to expand domestically, as well as in London and Tokyo. It completed 500,000 fully autonomous trips per week in March.

There's also Tesla (TSLA -7.64%), which is winding down the Cybertruck to shift factory production to the two-seater Cybercab. Its robotaxi service offers full self-driving rides in eight U.S. cities. Despite a slow rollout, Elon Musk has high hopes.

Assuming that both Waymo and Tesla make rapid advancements in AV tech, expand to new markets, grow their user bases, increase miles driven, lower costs, successfully navigate a patchy regulatory environment, and maintain strict safety standards, then it's possible their ride-hailing apps more effectively compete with Uber's leadership position in mobility.

And that's the threat that's harming sentiment toward Uber stock. There is terminal risk from AVs.

Uber is still operating at full strength. It ended the third quarter with 215 million monthly active users. Mobility gross bookings jumped 18% year over year to $29.5 billion. And operating income was well over $1.4 billion, up 30%.

The business has built up a powerful network effect, matching riders with drivers in cities around the globe. The key is that it directly controls demand and manages the customer relationship. That puts it in an advantageous position to essentially become the AV commercialization layer.

If you're an AV software developer, auto manufacturer, or any other related technical provider, you've likely incurred sizable capital investments. And it's preferred that monetization happens sooner rather than later. Plugging into Uber's platform seems like the best solution to maximize vehicle utilization. it completed 4.1 billion trips just in the last three months.

Management isn't waiting around. It's actively partnering with AV companies, most notably with a total planned $10 billion capital commitment, to drive supply onto its network. It already invested in Rivian Automotive, Lucid Group, and Nuro, for instance. And it's entered into car purchasing agreements.

The company is targeting about 700 fully driverless vehicles on its platform by year-end. By the end of 2027, it's aiming for more than 2,000. These are tiny figures in the grand scheme of things.

Uber believes that human drivers will also play a crucial role for a long time. This assumption is supported by the simple fact that rider demand fluctuates wildly depending on the time of day. Human drivers can handle demand spikes. If all rides were from AVs, on the other hand, many of these cars would be idle most of the time, which would be financially wasteful.

Still, it would be naive to think that Uber is in the clear. AVs pose a real long-term threat. It's something investors should monitor closely.

However, the business is leaning on its favorable hand by setting itself up to remain a leader in mobility. The current valuation more than reflects the market's negative view.

Want more control over your YouTube homepage? Try using custom feeds

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This tool allows you to search for exactly the type of content you want to see.

YouTube is a core hub of entertainment for millions of people. More than 20 million videos hit the platform every day, according to data from YouTube. That's a lot of content to sort through when you're trying to find something to watch. If you're a regular watcher of YouTube, your homepage is probably already leaning into your interests, be they comedy specials, home improvement tutorials or music videos from the '90s. But if you're looking to exert some more control and specificity over what the algorithm suggests to you, YouTube has a way to do it by offloading your playlist curation to AI.

As of October 2026, the platform introduced custom feeds. In short, custom feeds let you use natural language to set up feeds around the kind of content you want to see more of and keep it in a spot that's easy to access. Here's how to set up those feeds if you want to fine-tune your homepage.

If you want to tailor what you're seeing on YouTube more to your tastes, you can start by building a custom feed using prompts. At the top of your homepage, tap the + sign (you may also see the words "Custom feed"). From there, you can type into the box whatever you are interested in seeing. For example, YouTube gives examples like "I want retro tech nostalgia: 80s commercials, vintage PCs, CRT glow." Another example shows that you don't even have to get that specific. You could ask for a feed along the lines of, "I want to explore something completely different beyond my usual feed."

Once you create a feed, you'll find it at the top of the screen so you can access it when you want. YouTube noted that you can always use the prompt box at the top of a feed if you want to tweak what you're seeing.

If you like the idea of custom feeds, you can save up to three without a YouTube Premium account. If you do happen to have a YouTube Premium account, you can save up to eight. Regardless of your subscription, as long as you are signed into your account, the custom feeds you created will sync across mobile and desktop. It's also important to remember that custom feeds will expire if you don't use them for 30 days. If this happens, though, you can always create a new prompt. YouTube also pointed out that the feeds are subject to the platform's community guidelines.

So far, custom feeds are only available in English in the United States. You also need to have search history enabled to use the feature.

The rollout of custom feeds wasn't the only change YouTube brought to the homepage. Specifically on the mobile app, you might have noticed a row of feeds across the top of the page with categories like Live or Music. Some of these feeds might depend on your subscription. For example, you won't see the Movies & TV feed if you don't have any Primetime Channel subscriptions. Additionally, YouTube mentioned that the Subs feed, as in your subscriptions, moved to the top bar. If you want to exit your feeds, you can always tap on the Home tab on the left and go back to what you'd normally see on your homepage.

In its blogpost, YouTube said these changes are part of a series of updates to shape "a more seamless Home experience." So, if you're looking to create some feeds based on more specific interests that you can easily get to from the homepage, YouTube's custom feeds feature might be worth a prompt.

→ Baca artikel asli

High-end genealogy travel is booming, offering luxe journeys to the roots of your family tree

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The most exclusive itinerary is written in your DNA.

My great-great-grandfather came to the Tuscan ghost island of Pianosa as a prisoner. I arrived there on a luxury vacation.

The island lives up to its name, I thought as my ferry drifted toward the sole dock on this protected isola fantasma. “Pianosa” is derived from piano, Italian for “flat,” and indeed the island crouches low to the cyan Tyrrhenian Sea, its shores fringed by parasol pines and deserted prison watchtowers. From 1856 to 1998, Pianosa was a remote penal colony—and the place where my ancestor, Salvatore Giovanni Pellegrino Irace, died.

I’d learned this family lore thanks to a partnership between Ancestry, the Blackstone-owned leader in the at-home DNA-testing space, and Kensington, a bespoke travel outfitter, which invited me to test-drive its Personal Heritage Journey. After I submitted a DNA sample, Ancestry got to work filling in my Italian family tree, with researchers combing archives in Tuscany, Molise, and Campania. “So the interesting thing about Salvatore,” genealogist Kyle Betit said on our initial call, “is that he died in prison of tuberculosis in 1894—and we don’t know why he was there.”

Who doesn’t love a good family mystery? That’s part of what draws clients to Kensington’s genealogical-based trips. The journeys average $20,000 to $25,000 per person, with the company’s travel desk handling all the details in high style.

For me, that meant a chartered boat from mainland Tuscany to the island of Elba; chauffeured SUVs between regions; a private guide in Pompeii; and some spectacular accommodations, including Casa Angelina and Palazzo Avino on the Amalfi Coast. The major flex, though, was having a professional genealogist, Gabriele Musumeci, riding shotgun.

Once upon a time, possessing one’s comprehensive genealogy was a signal of wealth and pedigree. Then, in the 2000s, the rise of at-home DNA-testing kits from Ancestry and 23andMe made such knowledge commonplace. The ultrawealthy can now seek out deeper stories through first-person ancestry tourism, from China to Ghana. Kensington reports that demand has doubled and revenue has quadrupled in five years. Grand View Research put the global value of “heritage tourism”—of which ancestral travel is a segment—at $625 billion in 2025. It’s expected to reach almost a trillion by 2033.

“I think there’s a deep need in any of us to understand who we are,” Musumeci said as we descended the concrete steps to Pianosa’s white-sand beach, Cala Giovanna. “For most Americans, your story is cut.” He made a chopping motion. And then a sewing motion: “We stitch it back together.”

Most luxury travel planners now offer some variety of ancestry service. Alexandrea Padilha, director at the membership-based Fischer Unlimited, recently hired genealogists in Germany and the U.S. to trace a Louisiana tech entrepreneur’s ancestry. The results were bound into a book for the client’s father’s 80th birthday and also informed the itinerary for a trip to Germany—a way to show younger family members their roots. “I think that’s grounding for young adults today,” Padilha says, “living in a world that’s slowly slipping away.”

Existential dread is one hell of a motivator. “When it feels like the world may be shifting every second, people reach for something enduring,” says Dr. Katie Blake, a Texas-based social psychologist who specializes in travel. “The archives give you the facts, but it’s the place itself that gives you the identity pull that’s different than something you just see on paper.”

A sudden, biblical rainstorm broke open during our mile-long bike ride from Cala Giovanna to Pianosa’s imposing Agrippa Prison. We sheltered inside the Zante wing, where prisoners like Salvatore slept four to a concrete cell on metal beds, as thunder rattled the rusty gates.

My itinerary traced Salvatore’s life backward. A few days later, Musumeci and I rendezvoused in Molise, where Salvatore grew up. My great-great-great-grandfather, Vincenzo, moved his family to Montenero di Bisaccia, a mountain keep surrounded by vineyards and wheat fields sloping down to the Adriatic, in 1838, when Salvatore was a boy. At Montenero’s city hall, the clerks hauled out massive, crimson-bound books, and Musumeci leafed through until he found the birth and marriage records of Salvatore’s second son, Giuseppe—my great-grandfather.

Walking through Montenero’s hilltop puzzle of alleys brought those pieces of paper to life—and conjured a story of downward mobility. Along the town’s outer promenade, ringing the remains of medieval stone walls, a lookout faced the amber foothills where Vincenzo bought land and a farmhouse. I walked the narrow alley the family lived on after they lost the farm. Within four years, Vincenzo had spiraled from merchant and farm owner to street sweeper. He died in 1842, leaving his son on the poverty-to-prison pipeline.

I also saw the house in the old ghetto where my great-grandmother, Nicoletta, grew up. She and Giuseppe had a daughter out of wedlock, Mattia. (Nobody in my family had known Mattia existed until Musumeci discovered her.) The infant likely died during the couple’s 1900 journey to America, where, at Ellis Island, Irace became Erace. Giuseppe and Nicoletta settled in Philadelphia and had 16—yes, 16—more children, including my paternal grandfather, Michael Erace, who died in 2013.

It was like I was looking at Grandpop Mike’s long-lost brother when we met Bruno Irace, a 74-year-old Ironman triathlete and my fourth cousin, that afternoon. Smile, hair, complexion, bone structure; it was uncanny. We sipped espresso and compared photos, then visited a cemetery where “Irace” seemed to be engraved on every other gravestone. Two Iraces were listed on an obelisk in the village park honoring fallen soldiers. When it was time to leave Montenero, Bruno told us to come back soon. “You have family here,” he said.

The name Irace was also everywhere in the archives of San Luca, the family’s neighborhood church, in Praiano, on the Amalfi Coast, the final stop on my journey. This dramatic shoreline of improbable cliff-top villages and citrus groves set high above the sapphire sea is one of the most beautiful places in the world. I couldn’t help but wonder about the Iraces: Why did these fools leave? What misfortune befell my reasonably prosperous ancestors to make them abscond from this stunning seaside real estate?

Musumeci had coordinated with the pastor, who granted us free rein of his office, a charmingly cluttered room with sea views through diamond-paned windows. Hundreds of books, some dating to the 1600s, filled a tall cabinet, their yellowed pages brittle as autumn leaves. Handwritten in meticulous script were the births and baptisms, marriages and deaths of San Luca’s parishioners, which included generations of Iraces.

“Oh, wow … ” Musumeci stopped suddenly as his finger moved across a page. He pointed to Vincenzo’s marriage record. “Adam, the date … ” October 10, 1807.

It took a few seconds to dawn on me. I checked my phone: October 10, 2026. Exactly 219 years earlier, Vincenzo Irace and Mariangela Rispoli, ages 21 and 18, had stood at San Luca’s resplendent altar and exchanged vows. Until then, I’d encountered Vincenzo mostly through his losses: his farm, his livelihood, his family’s good name. Here was a record of a beginning. Whatever came later, my great-great-great-grandparents had first been two young people at the altar of a church above the sea.

I hadn’t found all the answers in Italy. We never learned why they left the Amalfi Coast, what sent their son to prison, or what compelled their grandson to cross an ocean. The private guides, chauffeured SUVs, and beautiful hotels couldn’t make the records yield answers they didn’t contain.

But the distance between my ancestors’ lives and my own no longer seemed quite so great. Standing in San Luca, I wasn’t the discerning traveler whose preferences shaped the itinerary. For all the choices I’d made on this trip, the ones that mattered most had been made two centuries earlier.

For once, the point wasn’t being made to feel important. The real privilege was the opportunity to feel very, very small.
