Figma vs. Fastly: Which Cloud Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Figma's revenue growth outpaces Fastly's by a wide margin, yet Fastly's lower multiples raise a sharper question about which story deserves your money.

By Brendan Coffey – Updated Nov 22, 2026 at 2:43PM EST

Rapid digital transformation is forcing organizations to prioritize speed and seamless team collaboration. Choosing between Figma (FIG -3.01%) and Fastly (FSLY -11.57%) requires weighing high-growth creative tools against specialized infrastructure.

Figma revolutionized the design process by moving collaboration into the browser, enabling teams to build digital products in real time. Fastly focuses on the delivery side, using its edge cloud to ensure those products reach users quickly and securely. Both companies provide essential services for the modern internet economy, making them frequent points of comparison for investors.

Figma provides a platform for teams to build digital products through collaborative design and prototyping. The company serves designers, developers, and collaborators across various industries, including a significant presence outside the U.S. In its latest annual report, the business highlighted a shift toward enterprisewide procurement and specialized programs like its education platform to drive deeper integration.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached about $1.4 billion, which represents a growth rate of approximately 44.4% compared to the prior year. This period saw a net loss of around $562.5 million. The growth trend among tech stocks like this highlights the current demand for specialized design software.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio is roughly 2.6x, indicating the company has sufficient short-term assets to cover its obligations. The debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.1x, and free cash flow reached about $246.2 million in the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 544% of operating cash flow, meaning reported cash generation is heavily inflated by this non-cash add-back.

Fastly operates an edge cloud platform designed to improve the speed and security of web applications. Major customers include media giants like TikTok, specifically the TikTok USDS Joint Venture LLC, and it maintains a commercial partnership with Comcast (CMCSA +0.16%). Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue was around $719.1 million, reflecting growth of roughly 21.5% over the previous period. During this time, the company reported a net loss of approximately $62.9 million. This performance suggests a stabilization in operating results as the company scales its delivery and security services.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio is roughly 2.6x, and the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.5x. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was around $65.8 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 124.2% of operating cash flow, meaning reported cash generation is heavily inflated by this non-cash add-back.

Figma faces intense competitive pressure from legacy players like Adobe Systems Inc. (ADBE -1.79%) and larger tech giants like Microsoft Corp. (MSFT -2.77%). The company is also navigating regulatory scrutiny, including a voluntary disclosure regarding potential export control issues. Managing rapid growth and the transition to usage-based pricing for AI features creates uncertainty around future revenue visibility.

Fastly carries significant customer concentration risk, as a large portion of its revenue depends on a few major clients. The company faces stiff competition from public cloud vendors like Amazon.com Inc. (AMZN -4.10%) and specialized rivals such as Cloudflare (NET -9.46%). Operational risks like service interruptions or supply chain delays for hardware components could also impact its ability to deliver performance.

Fastly appears to be the more value-oriented choice based on its lower multiples, while Figma trades at a premium that reflects its faster revenue expansion. The P/S ratio measures market capitalization against sales over the past twelve months. Meanwhile, the Forward P/E compares the share price to future earnings estimates for the coming year.

Valuation metrics are sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Figma has seen its user experience design solution become the industry standard for digital asset and mobile application development. The company uses this niche dominance to introduce additional modules, helping transform it from a single-point solution into a full design platform.

One differentiator from competitors is that Figma has done an excellent job not only presenting itself to developers, but also to marketers and product managers who have to collaborate on designs. That gives the company more champions in an organization for its products.

For the current fiscal year, revenue should grow quickly, at about 39%, to $1.47 billion. Look for a dramatic slashing of its net loss, from nearly $1.3 billion to about $560 million. Wall Street still sees similarly sized losses ahead for the foreseeable future, however.

Meanwhile, Fastly processes over 5 trillion requests a day, a huge number that is actually just a small part of the total addressable market (TAM) for the business. Management sees the TAM as $22 billion, giving it a long runway for growth. The company has posted three straight quarters of more than 20% growth (year-over-year).

One of the company's competitive edges, it says, is that it delivers its resources dynamically, allocating them in real time to whatever the global load is. So, for example, one Thursday night, its network might be delivering Thursday Night Football. On Black Friday, the exact same network is handling e-commerce. The next week, it might be handling a video game release. Fastly says that makes it a much more efficient network than competitors' siloed networks for security, compute, and delivery, et cetera.

For the current fiscal year, look for sales to grow to $747 million, a 20% rise over the prior year, with a much narrower net loss of about $56 million.

So which cloud tech stock is the better buy? Keep the decision simple by purchasing growth stocks at more reasonable prices. In this case, it is Fastly, which has the lower forward P/E and P/S ratios of the pair.

Sometimes, the best place for storage and charging is out of sight.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Multi-use devices that pull together disparate features often turn out to be junk. But it’s hard to knock a $9.89 device that combines an under-the-desk deadphone hanger / cable holder and a three-port 20W PD USB charging hub. Cozoo's device is basically a compact three-port charging brick with wings for hanging devices while they charge. It ships with both 3M tape and screws for mounting under your desk, as well as cable tie-downs to help make the setup as tidy as possible.

Mine has been stuck under my treadmill desk since, using the 3M tape option, and it shows no signs of falling off. Given the price (and the fact that we’ve never heard of the brand before), we wouldn’t expect the audio circuitry / cabling to be top-notch. But again, I've been using mine for nearly three years, and it's performed flawlessly so far.

This quirky device combines an under-desk headphone hook and a 20W PD USB charger with one USB-C and two USB-A ports. It brings a bit of extra convenience and tidiness to your desk setup. It comes in four colors and I've been using one under my standing desk since 2023.

With a pair of USB-A ports and USB-C, you can charge multiple devices at once. But the charger tops out at 20W, so don't expect to power your laptop here. It's also available in pink and white, but the color variants cost a few dollars more. Aside from holding my Sennheiser headphones when not in use, I also use the cable notches to keep track of the fast USB cables I need for external SSD testing.

Florian Wirtz shuts down Arsenal claim after Liverpool thrash Man Utd

📅 2026-11-22  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Florian Wirtz was quick to shut down any suggestion that Liverpool were spending too much time ‘thinking about’ Arsenal after their 4-0 win over Manchester United.

Mauricio Pochettino’s reign got off to a nightmare start in what was ultimately a mismatch between the two bitter rivals, with the Red Devils proving their own worst enemy and giving themselves a mountain to climb early on at Anfield.

Some calamitous defending from Matthijs de Ligt and Leny Yoro led to Dominik Szoboszlai’s tenth-minute breakthrough and saw the latter shown a straight red card by referee Samuel Barrott, a decision that only compounded the visitors’ woes.

The impressive Wirtz doubled Liverpool’s advantage with an audacious finish just three minutes later to send the home crowd into raptures, before a pair of second-half goals from Rio Ngumoha and Victor Munoz completed the rout.

Having cut a frustrated figure on the touchline for the most part, Pochettino made it clear he was less than impressed by the officiating on show as he faced the media shortly after the final whistle on Merseyside.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

The Argentinian will no doubt be desperate to turn the tide when United return to Premier League action at home to Brentford in six days’ time, following a midweek trip to Sporting.

The result ensured Pochettino’s men finished the weekend just one point better off than rock-bottom Crystal Palace, with the Old Trafford giants now leading Europe’s top five leagues for most goals conceded (34 in all competitions).

Liverpool, meanwhile, moved four points clear of fifth-placed Bournemouth and just one point behind Arsenal’s two nearest rivals, Manchester City and Brighton, after ten games.

‘Really good. The atmosphere after the game was so good. We could enjoy it and it was just a nice afternoon,’ Wirtz – named man of the match – said of the victory in his post-match interview with Sky Sports.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

‘I feel very good but it’s also because the team develops and gets better.

‘Just being part of this, you also get better, so I’m just enjoying that.’

Asked if he was perhaps benefitting from being coached by Andoni Iraola and Jurgen Klopp at club and international level, Wirtz replied: ‘Both are very good coaches and I enjoy playing under both.

‘They both bring different qualities and I’m just happy and can be happy to have two of these coaches.’

Victory in next Sunday’s Merseyside derby at Everton would see Liverpool move to within four points of Arsenal, who host Manchester City later that same afternoon.

When asked if the 4-0 thumping of United had taken Liverpool to ‘another level’, Wirtz replied: ‘Yeah, it was definitely important today, also for the table situation.

‘I think a lot of teams on top of the table, they didn’t win this weekend so it’s good that we could use the chance.

‘The red card influenced the game a bit of course but we take that, we earned it because of the run of Alex [Isak] so yeah, very good.’

Wirtz was then asked if he was ‘thinking about’ Arsenal and possibility of winning the league this season, and his answer was unequivocal.

It came after the Gunners – the reigning Premier League champions – surrendered a first-half lead to drop points away to Newcastle.

‘Uh, I wasn’t talking about Arsenal… all the teams, I don’t know, from one to three,’ Wirtz responded.

‘So we knew, we didn’t speak so much about it, but I think everybody knew it was a big chance to make some points.

Noah Kahan Deserved a Lot More Grammy Love Than Just One Minor Nomination. Here’s Why He Didn’t Get It

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Fans and professional forecasters alike were shocked when the superstar nearly got shut out, after predictions across the board that his 'Great Divide' blockbuster would earn major award recognition. Some of the reasons the Grammys didn't ultimately go for him may be in plain sight.

Senior Music Writer and Chief Music Critic

In previous eras of less pervasive social media, we usually could only guess at just how disappointed music and film personalities were when they didn’t fare well with Grammy or Oscar nominations. But lately, more and more major and minor figures have been up-front and honest in sharing with their fans that, hey, yes, they’re feeling the apparent lack of industry love, too. As long as these reflections come off as more bummed out and philosophical than bitterly entitled or peevish — here’s looking at you, John Summit — they can make for a moment of connection between artists and their fan base. And so it is that, this past week, I followed one of our most likably candid superstars through what I came to refer to as the Five Stages of Noah Kahan Grammy Grief.

Kahan himself skipped over the anger phase, though his fans certainly felt that one, when it turned out that their hero got passed over for everything but one relatively modest nom, in defiance of all expectation. There were other stages they went through together. First came uneasy anticipation: The day before nominations came out, Kahan issued a whatever-will-be-will-be-type tweet, almost as if he’d gotten a hint that things were not going to go well for him. (“Whatever happens tomorrow I love you all and I have done everything I’ve ever needed to do in my career because of YOUR support.”) Then came resignation and premature acceptance: “Life goes on.” Then came gallows humor, as he took a fake phone call on stage from the Grammys and quipped, “Wrong number.” And finally, there was real acceptance, as he admitted in an affecting stage monologue that he was having a teachable moment as, without mentioning the awards, Kahan said he was assessing why he had hoped too much for something and put the value of his worth in the wrong place.

Undoubtedly, some would say he should have just put himself above it all and not even referenced the seeming snub, but my admiration is always going to go more toward people who address elephants in rooms. So maybe now he could move forward with his usual good humor… while, sure, having the fact that he just sold out his next 24 U.S. stadium shows to keep him warm at night.

But maybe us Grammy prognosticators aren’t yet ready to move on, because like pollsters in 2024, we want to figure out how we got Kahan’s chances wrong. And really, he was the only significant thing we forecasted incorrectly, since virtually everything else in the top three categories went as predicted, for once. Kahan seemed to have almost everything going for him, being the only artist who consistently had not just one but two albums in the top 10 all year long, on top of having recently become one of the biggest ticket-sellers in the business… and, lest the predictions be based only on popular appeal, he’d also gotten mostly great reviews lately, although he hadn’t started out his career as a critical favorite.

Professional prognosticators nearly all had Kahan’s “The Great Divide” album landing one of the eight open slots for album of the year, and most entertainment news outlets predicted the same for the title track in the fields of record and/or song of the year. The forecasters lining up to say he was close to a sure thing included those at Billboard, Rolling Stone, the Hollywood Reporter, Hits and, yes, Variety. Over at the awards site Gold Derby, “Great Divide” was seen as all but locked in for an album nomination no matter which of their columns you looked down — industry experts, their editors, or the site’s active followers. Instead, he wound up with a lone nod for… best contemporary folk recording, which was rather like the Recording Academy setting a bull down in a china shop instead of at the rodeo. Related Stories Movie Marketing Veteran Gerry Rich on Opening 'Iron Man,' Grappling With AI and Maintaining the Human Touch How to Get Started on Making a Difference as Tax Rules for Charitable Giving Change

There was one obvious stumbling block on the path to considering him a shoo-in for one of more big nods: the fact that he’d only gotten two nominations before, and only one as the primary person nominated. Kahan was nominated as best new artist for the 2024 Grammys (and lost to the empirically less popular Victoria Monet); his only other prior nom was in a country category as the featured duet partner on a Kelsea Ballerini single. But surely we were past the point now — we thought — where anyone still had to dude-splain Noah Kahan to the Grammy elite.

Of course it shouldn’t matter if “The Great Divide” spent most of 2026 in the top 10 hovering just above the continued presence of the four-year-old (!) “Stick Season.” if these albums blew chunks… which is the kind of thing that’s been known to sink a Grammy nomination. But “The Great Divide” demonstrably does not fall into the realm of suckage. If anything, it represents the kind of perfect storm the Grammys have traditionally loved to reward, where somehow, some way, songwriting that is idiosyncratic and qualitatively strong and unique captures the imagination of the masses who might usually be stereotyped as tending toward less distinctly personal fare. Kahan could have been seen as having it all…

Except for having a natural constituency among Recording Academy members.

Therein lies a rub. It did nag at me, even as I was making my own forecast that he’d get into the top categories, that I couldn’t picture in my mind’s eye which subset of voters would be putting their support behind him. In terms of either genre or geography, he did not and does not have the wind at his back.

The Recording Academy has 12 chapters spread across the U.S. This may come as a shock, but none of them are in Burlington. There’s a Pacific Northwest chapter, but good luck looking for a corresponding Atlantic Northeast chapter. Surely Kahan would be the first to laugh at the idea of his New England industry homies colluding as a voting bloc to ensure vindication for Vermont.

The genre thing looms as an even bigger problem. Leaving aside the general field at the top of the ballot, where does a Kahan rightfully belong among the 90+ stylistically divided categories that follow? You could consider him rock. Billboard certainly does; he has two songs in the top 10 of the rock airplay chart right now (“Orbiter” and “Draw You Out”). But are the voters who choose to vote in the rock categories — and as you probably know, they don’t get to vote in all 100 — really going there to vote for a guy like Kahan, as opposed to the harder stuff? He would seem like a closer fit for the Americana and American roots categories. But the Americana community hasn’t really fully embraced him, even though he has shown up to their events. He didn’t come up through their farm system, and in a genre that deliberately favors underdogs, he might’ve simply gotten too popular too fast. So, “contemporary folk recording” it is, even if that is one oddball catch-all category. (But as has been pointed out, there are people nominated in that every year who have the nom as a highlight of their professional careers, so there should be no shame in it for Kahan, and he’d undoubtedly agree.)

If you really want to know why we all should have seen Kahan’s near-shutout coming, anyway, consider the ongoing case of Zach Bryan, who also falls between genres, despite also being one of the biggest recording and touring artists in the universe at the moment. I thought of Bryan as I was wondering whether Kahan would face the same problem with the Grammys, but at the time, I thought Bryan might have been snubbed most of all because of his surlier personality, whereas Kahan being an overtly personable guy would make the difference. Turns out they’re probably just in the same ballpark after all.

And then there is the issue of white guys with guitars, and how they’re doing, generally, in the culture, or with the Grammys specifically. The demographics the Recording Academy has been courting in expanding and diversifying its membership do not suggest they’re bringing in vast amounts of people who are going to put a Kahan at the top of their ballot, any more than they’re going to vote for Paul McCartney (whose critically hailed “Boys of Dungeon Lane” would have been a shoo-in for top noms with the Grammy voters of the 2000s, but not the 2020s). When the Grammys invited 3,600 pros to join as voting members in the class of 2025, 56% were people of color and 60% were under 40. You don’t look at those stats and imagine that any of the various waning subgenres of rock are suddenly going to be overrepresented among the biggest Grammy nominees.

But here’s the other thing to take into account: all those new voters, along with the older ones the Academy has not aged it out, are getting it right more often than not. As much as I like “The Great Divide,” when I look at the album of the year lineup to see which nominee I would personally knock out to make room for it, there are only a couple of the eight that seem even remotely expendable. Most forecasters would probably say that Phoebe Bridgers’ “Lost Weekend” took a slot that was expected to go to Kahan, and maybe she knocked him down to ninth place, although we’ll never know for sure where he ended up. There’s absolutely nothing regrettable about her making it in, or the utterly deserving Rosalía or Raye or most of the others… and obviously no shame in women earning seven out of eight noms in some of the top categories, after the “step up, girls” era, and on merit. Of all the years for any sense of overriding Grammy outrage to take hold, this is not one of them.

But I can think of other artists besides Kahan I think got “robbed” in the top categories, like Sienna Spiro, who should have been up for all four top general-field trophies but is only in for best new artist. A year this solid and action-packed makes the case that the Academy should considering reupping the number of finalists in the top four categories from eight to 10, as was the case in 2022 and 2023 before tjeu dropped it back down again. If the Oscars can stick with 10 nominees at the top of the ballot, so could the Grammys.

In the meantime, as Kahan fans lick wounds, they ought to be able to take some comfort in the hundred factors that make even a worthy contender get shut out, or nearly ignored. It’s not him, it’s them — in all sorts of ways — and there are real reasons to believe that, not just the delusional ones hardcore stans come up with to justify more negligible artists being slighted. And if the reasons for being left out have to do with being the kind of artist who snuck up completely between the cracks, and who’s thriving beyond belief, even as he continues to be a little bit stepped over, that’s a triumph that might finally be even better than a Grammy.

Alexander Skarsgård’s $10M Fantasy Romance Officially Nears 4x Its Budget

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

Having spent nearly a month in theaters, a sleeper hit fantasy romance film starring Olivia Colman and Alexander Skarsgård is all set to begin a new chapter in its journey at home. This week, the movie will be released on the PVOD market, along with Aaron Sorkin's box-office underperformer The Social Reckoning. No one would have seen this coming, but the fantasy romance movie has outperformed The Social Reckoning by a wide margin, having nearly quadrupled its production budget in theaters. The movie will no doubt have a solid run at home, given its positive reviews and stellar audience reception.

That said, the movie's box-office run appears to be drawing to a close. It has witnessed a significant drop in revenue over the last couple of weeks, as a result of a fast-declining theater count. In its fifth week of release, with a digital debut approaching, the movie is expected to see further declines in both revenue and its theatrical footprint. Also featuring Elizabeth Debicki and Peter Dinklage, the film has emerged as the first major box-office hit distributed domestically by Black Bear Pictures.

The firm had previously released the Jason Statham-led action movie Shelter and Guy Ritchie's costly misfire In the Grey this year. Shelter grossed around $13 million domestically, while In the Grey emerged as Ritchie's lowest-grossing film in over two decades, with a final haul of under $6 million.

Four love stories, four completely different ideas of what it means to be somebody's person. This is the one that matches how you actually fall, and what it would ask of you.

You do not fall for strangers. You fall for the person you have already been talking to for years, which is the slowest and by some distance the most durable way anybody has found to do this.

You want a partner, not an audience. Somebody who wants their own life as badly as you want yours, and who would never ask you to be smaller to make the relationship easier.

The intensity was never the problem. The secrecy is. You are drawn to a love that lives entirely in private, and to the particular ache of wanting somebody you are not supposed to want.

You do not do this cautiously. When it happens for you it is immediate and total and it rearranges everything, and you would not accept a smaller version of it.

Black Bear's new movie, the fantasy romance starring Colman and Skarsgård, is Wicker. Directed by Alex Huston Fischer and Eleanor Wilson, the film holds a "Certified Fresh" 94% critics' score and a "Verified Hot" 92% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes. The aggregator website's consensus reads, "Wicker is a charming fable that strikes the right balance between naughty and nice, drawing much of its appeal from deftly deployed salty humor, seamless creature effects, and Olivia Colman's eminently lovable performance."

The movie exceeded box-office expectations in its opening weekend of release and showed solid resilience until its theater count began to fall by week three. Its global haul now stands at nearly $40 million against a reported budget of $10 million. At its peak, Wicker was playing in 2,374 locations nationwide. But it lost more than 1,200 theaters over the last couple of weeks. By the time it enters its fifth week in theaters, it will have lost more than 1,500 locations overall. The movie is expected to debut on digital platforms on November 24, alongside The Social Reckoning and A24's sensational You Can See Everything. Stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

Wicker is a film set in a coastal fishing village where an isolated woman crafts a husband from wicker. Released in 2026, the narrative explores themes of loneliness and creativity as the protagonist seeks companionship through her handmade creation.

How long is a new Fitbit actually supposed to last?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Longevity is a major factor to consider with any fitness wearable. It's meant to be used 24/7, and its subject to conditions that would wreck many other products, from accidental knocks and scrapes to heavy deadlift sessions. As gadgets go, there are few things worse than discovering you're out a few hundred dollars because your tracker wasn't sufficiently sweatproof.

There's more to longevity than durability, though. There's also battery health, not to mention software support, since a tracker is useless if you can't sync and browse its data. At some point, end of support is virtually guaranteed — it's just a question of whether that will be before or after you're ready to move on.

How long can you expect a new product like the Fitbit Edge or Fitbit Air to last? While we obviously can't say for certain, info from Google, third parties and my own personal experience with wearables may be helpful.

Let's return to durability for a moment. Most impacts are only going to do cosmetic damage, as long as you don't destroy the screen or button(s), so the greater concern is water resistance. A product like the Edge has an IP68 rating, meaning it can be submerged and keep running, but that's only for as long as its liquid protection seals hold up.

Over time, the seals on any device are bound to degrade, especially with repeated exposure to moisture. It's just a question of how quickly. While a person who only wears their Fitbit for walking might never run into trouble, someone who swims daily might encounter a sudden breakdown a few years down the road. Sweat killed three generations of my Beats earbuds, despite their supposed IPX4 sweat and water resistance.

As for battery health, all lithium ion batteries have a finite number of 0 to 100 percent charge cycles. In wearables, this is typically about 300, so charging a device roughly once per week could in theory get you over five years of runtime. That's not the whole picture, however. Apart from variable cycle counts, your device's health can be prolonged if you rarely let your battery go dead, but its lifespan can also be harmed by the issue of practical capacity.

Before you hit that 300 mark, various chemical factors will probably reduce capacity to the point you no longer consider a Fitbit usable. Most people don't want to charge a wearable multiple times per day. Also, while the Edge has a replaceable battery, it's the exception to the rule. There's no choice but to dispose of most Fitbits once they cross this threshold.

Google explains that it offers security updates for at least two years after a Fitbit product is no longer on sale. If you buy one of the company's current models, updates are going to last through 2028 and probably beyond, depending on when Google refreshes its hardware. The Inspire 3, Versa 4 and Sense 2 will presumably be first on the chopping block, since they launched in 2022.

Note that security updates aren't the same thing as active feature support. Companies like Google tend to prioritize the newest hardware, both to encourage you to spend money, and because older products can only keep up with feature changes for so long. For example, Google Health Guardian features like irregular rhythm notifications only work with a narrow range of Fitbits, despite some unsupported models having heart rate sensors too. Meanwhile, the original Fitbit app has been replaced by Google Health. There's no telling how long legacy devices will remain compatible as the new app evolves.

You may eventually find features so basic or broken that you end up buying a new device regardless of damage, battery health or security vulnerabilities. Realistically, I wouldn't count on owning a new Fitbit for any more than five years, but I hope I'm proven wrong.

Investor Bill Miller Says Bitcoin Is Easier to Value than AI

📅 2026-11-22  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Famous Bitcoin (BTC) advocate and investor Bill Miller IV said that it is much easier to value the world's largest cryptocurrency than AI investments, even though Bitcoin does not produce any cash flow.

He said that this has to do with the expectations investors have when it comes to these two assets, specifically regarding their capacity to deliver future profits.

In a recent X post, Miller, who is the chairman and chief investment officer of Miller Value Partners, broke down why he thinks that it's easier to make an investment case for Bitcoin than for AI companies.

"Bitcoin and AI owners have different expectations. The owners of AI expect companies will one day produce free cash flow. Bitcoin owners never did or will expect this," Miller said.

And the end result is that "the valuation case is still much easier for Bitcoin than AI."

Simply put, when investors buy stocks in AI companies, there's an expectation that at some point these companies will deliver big profits and free cash flow.

Free cash flow is the money a company produces from its operations after capital expenditures. This is a significant metric for understanding the value of a company.

But when it comes to AI companies, it can be difficult to assign a value because investors need to try to determine what kind of revenue these companies will generate, how much they will invest in infrastructure and when these investments will start to deliver sufficient returns.

Bill Miller has made it a point to push back against the argument that Bitcoin does not have value because it doesn't produce cash flow.

In a commentary from June 30, Miller explained that the traditional way of looking at intrinsic value, which revolves around future cash flows, doesn't really capture the potential of Bitcoin as a decentralized monetary network.

Instead, Miller said that Bitcoin's value could be determined by comparing its potential role within the global financial system to that of gold.

In his view, Bitcoin could theoretically reach about $1.4 million per coin if it achieved a valuation similar to gold's. At least, that's what he calculated at the time.

Here's when a Raspberry Pi 5 could be considered overkill

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The ongoing memory shortage has led to price increases across the tech industry, even affecting historically affordable computers like the Raspberry Pi. In early November 2026, Raspberry Pi CEO Eben Upton announced that the latest Raspberry Pi 5's 16GB variant was getting an $80 price hike. At $385 for the 16GB Pi 5, it's safe to say that the platform is no longer as affordable as it once was. The Pi 5's 2GB, 4GB and 8GB models saw price increases earlier in 2026 too.

But if you're looking to buy a Raspberry Pi today, you don't have to limit yourself to the newest Pi 5 board. Depending on your intended use case, you could save significant money by buying an older model. The Pi 4, in particular, is still a capable platform with plenty of power for both light desktop use and the typical projects you would run on a Pi. And you're not just saving on the board itself — the Pi 4 has more modest power and cooling requirements than the latest model.

The Raspberry Pi 5 is more than twice as powerful as the previous-generation Pi 4, but you'll only notice that difference in the real world if you use it as your sole computer. The Raspberry Pi 4's slower CPU means it's not as well-suited for web browsing or heavy workloads. But if you're planning to use the board as a springboard for hobbies like self-hosting, home automation or robotics, you're unlikely to find the Pi 4's performance lacking.

Unfortunately, the higher-end Raspberry Pi 4 models have also seen substantial price increases over the past year or so. Still, they remain slightly cheaper than the Pi 5 and more widely available. The Pi 4's official starting price is just $35 for the 1GB model, the same as the original Raspberry Pi that was released in 2012.

The Pi 4's less performant CPU also saves you money on power and cooling. It only needs a 15W USB adapter, which is far more common than the 27W USB-C PD adapter that the Pi 5 required. Additionally, the older Pi 4 runs cooler, and you don't need to add a cooling fan to the bill of materials for most projects. However, the Raspberry Pi 5 tends to exceed 176 degrees Fahrenheit (80 degrees Celsius) after a minute of heavy load. Without a cooling fan, the board then throttles the CPU to bring temperatures under control.

When you factor in the cost of a more capable power adapter and cooling hardware, the Pi 5 ends up costing more than the $45 starting price would have you believe. The Pi 4's CPU also benefits from cooling hardware, but it doesn't get hot as quickly and you can get away with a cheap, passive aluminum heatsink.

If you're willing to stomach the extra initial cost of a Raspberry Pi 5 and hunt for available stock, it's definitely the more future-proof option. The Pi 5's 1GB and 2GB RAM variants, in particular, are only slightly more expensive than the Pi 4, but whether those work for you depends on how much RAM you need from your Pi.

That said, the advantage to buying an older Raspberry Pi is availability. Many official retailers simply cannot keep the affordable Pi 5 models in stock, while you can easily buy a Pi 4 or 3B+ without waiting for a restock. The $35 Pi 4 is also available at Micro Center locations in the US, while you'd have to order the base model Pi 5 online and pay for shipping.

The used market is also flooded with older Pi 4 boards as people upgrade to either the latest model or a more powerful computer altogether. In my city, I see dozens of listings for Raspberry Pi 4 and 3B+ boards on Facebook Marketplace, all selling well below the current retail price. These deals often include power supplies, cases and other necessary accessories too.

Raspberry Pi boards are safe to buy second-hand as they're surprisingly durable. They don't have any moving parts and most people use them for relatively lightweight loads. Even if you use a Raspberry Pi as a media server, for instance, the board remains idle most of the time. With minimal power draw and heat generation, a Pi can easily last several years if not a decade. So buying a used Raspberry Pi isn't as much of a gamble as a pre-owned desktop.

Raspberry Pi's CEO has called the recent price increases a "difficult, but temporary" phase and committed to lowering prices when memory costs fall. However, memory manufacturers have warned of supply shortages extending into 2028 and beyond. Put simply, the latest Raspberry Pi may come down in cost anytime soon.

Employee Health Benefits End at Foundation Future Industries

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CNBC reported on November 22, 2026, citing an internal memo it obtained, that Foundation Future Industries told employees their health benefits through administrator Rippling had been terminated retroactively to October 2026 as the humanoid-robot startup missed payrolls and sought emergency financing.

The staff email told employees they would be offered COBRA coverage for November 2026, with Foundation promising to reimburse premiums and onboarding a new benefits provider. COBRA is the federal law that generally lets workers temporarily keep health insurance after losing benefits by paying the full premium out of pocket.

The memo blamed Rippling for the disruption and cited Foundation’s last pay run through the administrator in October as the reason benefits ended retroactively. Rippling said Foundation had struggled to meet payroll and benefits obligations for months. Under its Professional Employer Organization policy, clients that cannot meet those obligations for multiple months first receive a warning and are terminated if the obligations remain unmet.

People with direct knowledge of the situation said employees were concerned Foundation might lack the money to reimburse premiums or restore coverage, particularly when family members were undergoing medical treatment. That risk lands on workers who have already gone without pay and may need to cover the full premium themselves while waiting for reimbursement.

Foundation’s financial difficulties began in August 2026, and it has been seeking a bridge loan to repay employees. Co-founder and CEO Sankaet Pathak told staff the company had held talks with three or four venture capital firms, including existing investor Tribe Capital, according to people familiar with the situation.

Pathak described the bridge loan as a way to keep Foundation operating until a possible $150 million investment from South Korean conglomerate Base Group arrives, potentially as early as December 2026 or January 2027. The investment has not been finalized. The gap between that timetable and employees’ immediate needs makes the bridge loan central to Foundation’s effort to repay staff.

Founded in 2024, Foundation is pursuing humanoid robots for military and industrial work instead of household tasks and service-sector roles. Eric Trump has invested in the company and holds the title of chief strategy advisor.

Employees were last paid on October 15, 2026. With another payday scheduled for November 30, 2026, workers face a third consecutive pay cycle without income. Foundation continued hiring during its financial difficulties, and some new workers have never been fully paid, people familiar with the company said.

Many employees are drawing down savings to cover living expenses and actively interviewing for other jobs, while attendance at Foundation’s San Francisco headquarters has fallen sharply. “I’m burning through savings just to afford my apartment,” one employee told CNBC.

Pathak used an all-hands meeting to address employee anger over a $4.6 million loan he took from Foundation in 2025. He defended the arrangement by saying he had put his own money into the startup and took only a nominal salary.

Pathak has floated offering equity to discourage departures before a possible Base investment. He also told employees facing severe financial difficulty to contact human resources for help covering personal bills. At the same time, human resources staff warned workers they would be dismissed if they filed complaints with California to recover their wages, people familiar with the situation said.

The result is a financing problem with immediate consequences for employee retention and health coverage. Staff are waiting for missed wages and facing insurance costs while Foundation pursues funding that has not been secured, leaving its humanoid-robot plans dependent on emergency cash and its workforce absorbing the delay.

Nonprofits Build Capacity for Potential Giving After Anthropic’s IPO

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Associated Press reported on November 22, 2026 that nonprofits are preparing for potential donations from Anthropic employees and founders following the AI company’s IPO. The co-founders’ pledges were valued at $112 billion based on the IPO price, but many employees cannot sell or gift most shares until spring 2027, so nonprofits are expanding capacity without counting on immediate donations.

The report put Anthropic’s debut valuation at a record $1.5 trillion. Its seven co-founders have pledged to donate 80% of their equity, while employee wealth has drawn attention from organizations working on global health, animal welfare and scientific research.

In an October 18, 2026 announcement, Anthropic said its proposed Public Benefit Fund would initially receive 5% of company shares, subject to stockholder approval, with another 2% available at the board’s discretion for every 50% increase in company value above a set baseline. The LLC structure preserves flexibility among forms of giving, with lobbying and political giving excluded from use of its Anthropic equity. Approximately half of employees participate in a separate equity donation match program.

GiveDirectly offers a concrete example of what that preparation entails. President and CEO Nick Allardice has spent months identifying barriers to expanding direct cash transfers, which send money to poor households without conditions on its use. Recipients need mobile phones for digital payments, so the nonprofit secured investments during 2026 to build a larger phone supply chain. It offers phones for about $15, generally deducting the cost from a recipient’s first payment.

A September 21, 2026 operational plan set a goal of being able to absorb $5 billion or more annually by 2028, ten times GiveDirectly’s capacity at the end of 2025. Existing operations could deliver approximately $500 million during 2026, already more than the organization had in donations to distribute. The plan does not assume that its target will be funded.

The preparation investments are funded by private donors rather than general donations and address bottlenecks that cash cannot immediately solve: regulatory approvals, strategic partnerships, technology and suppliers. GiveDirectly distributed nearly 125,000 phones in 2025 and is preparing for a possible need for 2 million to 3 million annually. Its procurement work includes direct negotiations with manufacturers, real-time inventory tracking and recruitment of a global phone procurement manager.

Remote poverty identification, identity verification and recipient communication could also expand reach without proportional staff growth. GiveDirectly’s plan cautions that reducing in-person contact can create fraud, safeguarding and exclusion risks, particularly for people without phones or experience using mobile money. The operational challenge extends beyond phone procurement to preserving verification, trust and access as enrollment becomes less labor-intensive.

Some grantmaking is already accelerating in anticipation of broader AI-related giving. On July 23, 2026, Coefficient Giving announced that it and Good Ventures were increasing their 2026 commitment to GiveWell’s recommendations from $175 million to $1 billion. The one-off surge, which does not establish a new steady state, is intended to fill cost-effective gaps and help grantees hire, expand geographically and build capacity for future donors. That bet has a clear limitation: much of the anticipated future funding depends on the valuations of extremely volatile assets and might not materialize.

Wild Animal Initiative executive director Cameron Meyer Shorb expects considerable fresh support for animal advocacy and sees an important role for midsize donors. The nonprofit, which funds wild animal welfare research, has connected with a couple of Anthropic employees and is building relationships with philanthropic advisers. Its outreach remains gradual and restrained because newly wealthy donors can face intense solicitation from anyone able to reach them.

Renaissance Philanthropy president Kumar Garg, whose nonprofit matches funders with scientific initiatives, emphasized the execution problem: “The challenge is that deploying philanthropic capital is hard. That’s not an automatic step.” For these organizations, delivery systems need to be built before large gifts arrive, while the gifts themselves depend on locked-up shares, volatile asset values and donor choices that operational preparation cannot settle.

ILLA Builder's 92 Fingerprints Mark a Low-Code Control Plane With Data Connections

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ILLA Builder is designed to assemble internal tools from visual components, data sources, and actions. That makes the builder more consequential than a static dashboard. A reachable deployment can hold database connections, API credentials, and the authority to publish applications to other users.

On 22 November 2026, ZoomEye SDK probes used sub_type=all, page 1, and pagesize=1. app="ILLA Builder" returned 92 matches, title="ILLA Builder" returned 92, and http.body="ILLA Builder" returned 109. The probes completed between 06:46 and 06:47 Asia/Shanghai.

The close agreement between app and title is a useful signal. The body query adds 17 more candidates, which may include pages that mention the product without placing the name in the HTML title. None of these totals proves that an instance is unauthenticated or that its data sources are reachable.

The project describes itself as a low-code platform for internal tools such as dashboards, CRUD applications, admin panels, CRM, and CMS. It documents connectors for relational databases, REST APIs, GraphQL, MongoDB, Redis, Hugging Face, and other services.

Those connections are the point of the product. They are also the reason an exposed builder needs an ownership review. A user who can change a query, action, or resource may be able to read more data than the published application suggests. The builder process may hold credentials that are never visible in the browser.

Self-hosting with Docker, Docker Compose, or Kubernetes adds another question: which environment variables, mounted secrets, and service accounts does the deployment use? A test instance with sample data has a different impact from a production builder attached to customer systems.

For each owned deployment, record the base URL, authentication mode, user roles, workspace membership, data-source connections, outbound network access, and published applications. Separate the builder console from the applications end users are meant to see.

Require strong authentication for the builder and place administrative routes on a private network. Give each connector a least-privilege credential. Do not reuse a database administrator account for application previews or scheduled actions. Test that a user without builder rights cannot enumerate connections or execute an action directly.

Monitor outbound requests from the builder and its action workers. A REST connector can reach internal services if the network policy allows it. Apply an egress allowlist that includes only approved data sources.

The 92 application matches and 92 title matches provide a coherent product signal. The 109 body matches show why a second field can improve discovery. They still do not show authentication state, ownership, data-store reach, or software version. Those facts belong in the asset review.

Is This Height Comparison Up to Scale? How to Draw One Correctly in JavaScript and SVG

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"I've always wondered what a height different between a 4'10 person and a 5'7 person would look like … I found this image but idk if it's up to scale or not."

That question, posted to r/heightcomparison, is the whole genre in one sentence. People want to see a height gap, they find a picture, and then they have no way to tell whether the picture is honest. If you are building the tool that makes those pictures, "is it up to scale?" is the one question your code has to answer correctly every time.

It sounds trivial: multiply height by a constant and draw. In practice, height comparison charts break in a handful of predictable ways: an axis that does not start at zero, figures that float instead of standing on a floor, decorations that add fake height, and a sneaky unit-conversion bug that shifts some heights by a quarter of an inch and not others. This post builds a small, framework-free JavaScript module for a height comparison to scale, with a node --test suite. Along the way it explains each failure mode and the test that catches it.

The original question is old, but the pattern keeps repeating. In September 2026 a developer posted their new height comparison site to the same subreddit and asked for brutally honest feedback, specifically: "Does the visual height difference look intuitive?" and "Do you notice anything that looks inaccurate or misleading?" The replies are a free QA report, and three of them are worth turning into requirements:

On the developer side, the parsing questions never stop. A Stack Overflow question asks how to convert 5'5" to centimetres when the data sometimes says 5' 5" with a space. An r/learnjavascript beginner tried to convert feet and inches to metres and lost the values to variable scope. And in r/FanFiction, someone wanted to line up 25 characters and found that every site capped the number of people.

So the requirements write themselves: accept the formats people type, refuse the ones that are ambiguous, never let a conversion drift, draw everyone on one floor with one scale, and be honest about what a silhouette can and cannot show.

Is this height comparison up to scale? The same 4'10" and 5'7" drawn three ways. Only the third panel, with an axis from zero and one shared ground line, is a height comparison to scale.

A height comparison to scale has exactly two rules:

The truncated axis is the same mistake as a bar chart that does not start at zero. It is tempting because it makes small differences "readable," but in a height chart the visual ratio is the message. If you need to emphasize a 2 cm gap, add a zoomed inset or a number, not a cropped axis.

Height comparison to scale in JavaScript: storing Math.round(cm) and converting back to the nearest quarter inch shifts exactly the odd heights from 5'11" to 6'7" by 1/4 inch. Storing unrounded centimetres gives zero drift.

Let's start with the bug report, because it is the most instructive. I don't know how that site is built, but a very common pipeline reproduces the report exactly:

Run that for every inch from 5'11" to 6'7", and the heights that drift are 5'11", 6'1", 6'3", 6'5" and 6'7". Those are the odd ones, just as reported, with 6'5" going up a quarter and 6'1" going down a quarter (185.42 → 185 → 72.835 in → 6'0¾"). Even heights in that range happen to land within an eighth of an inch after rounding, so they look fine, which is exactly why this bug survives casual testing.

The inch is defined as exactly 2.54 cm, so there is no reason to lose information. The fix is a rule: pick one canonical unit, store it unrounded, and round only at the display edge. Centimetres as a JavaScript Number is fine; a double holds 180.34 to far more precision than a chart needs. If you persist to a database, an integer number of micrometres or a decimal column also works. Whole centimetres do not.

Height inputs arrive as 5'11", 5' 11, 5ft 11in, 71 in, 180 cm, 1.8 m, and the one that causes real trouble: 5.11. Is that 5.11 feet (about 155.8 cm) or 5 feet 11 inches (180.34 cm)? Users mean the second far more often, but silently guessing is how a 25 cm error ends up in a chart. My parser refuses it and offers a suggestion the UI can show as "Did you mean 5'11"?":

The 20–300 cm range is a product policy, not a fact about people. It matches the limits on Body Visualizer's height comparison tool, which also requires whole feet and inches below 12. Pick limits that suit your audience and test both edges.

Displaying feet and inches has its own classic bug: 5'12". If you compute feet with Math.floor and then round the remaining inches, 71.9 inches becomes 5 feet and 12 inches. Round the total first, then split:

denominator: 4 gives quarter inches; denominator: 1 gives whole inches. Because the input is the unrounded canonical value, formatFtIn(parseHeight("6'5\"").cm, { denominator: 4 }) returns 6'5" every time.

The difference readout should follow the same rule. I compute the percentage relative to the shorter person, which is how the Body Visualizer tool explains it too: 160 cm vs 175 cm is a 15 cm gap, and 15 / 160 = 9.375%. Whichever base you choose, state it in the UI, because "9.4% taller" and "8.6% shorter" describe the same pair.

The layout function is where "up to scale" is actually enforced. It computes a single pxPerCm from the tallest person plus some headroom, puts 0 cm on the ground line, and measures every figure from the same floor:

niceStep picks ruler intervals of 1, 2, 2.5 or 5 times a power of ten, so a chart of people gets 25 cm ticks and a chart that includes a building gets round hundreds:

The layout function is pure: give it heights and a box, get numbers back. That makes the "to scale" property directly testable, which matters more than any amount of visual review.

If you need many people, like the 25 fan-fiction characters, keep the same pxPerCm and let the chart scroll horizontally. Shrinking each slot until figures overlap, or splitting the cast into separately scaled charts, breaks comparability. If you do split, reuse one scale across every chunk.

Here is the subtle part behind the "circle" complaint. Most silhouette artwork has padding: transparent space above the head inside the image or SVG viewBox. Scale that asset to heightPx and the visible crown lands below the true height. Add a portrait circle above the head and the eye reads the circle's top as the person's top.

My silhouette path is authored in a unit box where y = 0 is the crown and y = 1 is the soles, with nothing above or below:

translate to the crown, then scale by the pixel height, and the feet land exactly on groundY. If you use richer artwork, measure its real bounding box once (for example with getBBox() in a build step) and normalize it to this convention. Keep names, portraits and badges outside the height box: below the ground line, beside the figure, or on the chest as that commenter suggested. The full renderSvg also draws the ruler, escapes names (people will type into a name field), and adds a so screen readers get the heights as text.</p> <p>The 195.5 cm user who expected the figure's eyes at 6 ft has a fair point, but it reveals a limit rather than a bug. A generic silhouette puts the eyes at a fixed fraction of its drawn height. A real person's eye height depends on their head size and posture, which you have not measured. If you draw an "eye line," say it is approximate and derived from the figure, not from the user.</p> <p>Body Visualizer's height comparison page takes a clear position here: the drawing "shows relative total height only" and "cannot establish where someone's head would reach on another person's body, because individual proportions have not been measured." That is a good sentence to borrow for your own UI. The same honesty applies to width. A height chart that draws a heavier person as a wider silhouette is guessing. If you want to show build, use a tool built for that, such as a side-by-side height and weight comparison in 3D, and label it as an estimate.</p> <p>The suite has nine tests. These are the ones I'd keep even if I deleted everything else:</p> <p>The others cover parsing (5'11", 5ft 11in, 1.8 m, typographic quotes), refusals (5'13", a bare 180, 400 cm, the ambiguous 5.11 with its 5'11" suggestion), carry (5'11.9" → 6'0"), the 9.375% difference in both input orders, ruler steps, and an SVG check that each crown's translate equals its computed topY. Run them with node --test; there are no dependencies.</p> <p>The round-trip test is the one that would have caught the bug from the Reddit thread. It walks 329 quarter-inch heights through parse → store → format and demands the same string back. The first test documents the failure mode, so nobody "optimizes" storage back to whole centimetres later.</p> <p>"Is it up to scale?" has a precise answer: same floor, same pixels per centimetre, axis from zero, and numbers that survive a round trip without drifting. Everything else (portraits, presets, PNG export) is decoration, and decoration should never move a crown by a pixel.</p> <p>If you want to compare your own result against a working implementation, Body Visualizer's height comparison draws two to six people on one shared scale and ground line and exports PNG or SVG. Its height simulator previews a single height on a 3D model with fixed proportions. Like any silhouette, both show entered heights, not measurements of real people. Nothing here is a health assessment; it's arithmetic and geometry, done carefully.</p> <p>DEV Community — A space to discuss and keep up software development and manage your software career</p> <p>Built on Forem — the open source software that powers DEV and other inclusive communities.</p> <p>Made with love and Ruby on Rails. </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> </div> </article> <article class="article-card" id="artikel-5498"> <script type="application/ld+json">{"@context": "https://schema.org", "@type": "NewsArticle", "headline": "Karpathy's 'Second Brain' Pattern, Applied to Obsidian and Claude Code", "image": ["https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F03fgeomg2d6t4dsenqu9.png"], "datePublished": "2026-11-21T23:40:09", "dateModified": "2026-11-21T23:40:09", "mainEntityOfPage": "https://railfansday.github.io/berita1jam/technology.html#artikel-5498"}</script> <h2>Karpathy's 'Second Brain' Pattern, Applied to Obsidian and Claude Code</h2> <div class="article-meta"> 📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: <strong>Technology</strong>  |  <a href="https://dev.to/dimsbuild/karpathys-second-brain-pattern-applied-to-obsidian-and-claude-code-138j" target="_blank" rel="nofollow">Read Source →</a> </div> <div class="article-body"> <img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F03fgeomg2d6t4dsenqu9.png" alt="Karpathy's 'Second Brain' Pattern, Applied to Obsidian and Claude Code" loading="lazy"> <div style="text-align:center; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>Posted on Nov 21 • Originally published at dimitriaigle.fr</p> <p>On April 3, 2026, Andrej Karpathy posts an idea that goes massively viral: stop using an AI agent for one-off questions, and point it at a folder of sources so it builds a personal wiki that grows with every addition. The principle holds up, and the real structure goes further than "three folders and a prompt": immutable sources, a generated wiki, an action log, and a periodic verification pass. Budget 5 minutes for an empty vault, 60 to 90 minutes to migrate an existing knowledge base.</p> <p>Andrej Karpathy, a founding member of OpenAI and former AI director at Tesla, posted the idea on X: instead of asking an agent to write code or answer a one-off question, point it at a folder and let it build a living knowledge base. The post passed 19 million views and got picked up, tested, and documented across the AI community within weeks.</p> <p>A note you write yourself captures what you thought you understood at the moment of writing it. A note the agent writes, from a raw source (an article, a transcript, a PDF), captures what the source actually says, and updates itself when a new source completes or contradicts it. The agent doesn't replace your thinking, it keeps the wiki current while you keep feeding it sources.</p> <p>The versions that spread on social media reduce it to three elements: a raw sources folder, a wiki folder, a CLAUDE.md file. The structure documented in detail goes further:</p> <p>Two rules set this system apart from an ordinary notes folder: the sources/ folder stays immutable, which lets you check later what a source originally said. The wiki/ folder is only ever modified by the agent, never by hand: fixing an error means adjusting the instructions or adding a source, not editing the output directly.</p> <p>Five minutes is enough to get started on an empty vault: create the folders, write the CLAUDE.md, drop in a first source. On a knowledge base already filled with hundreds of files, migration takes longer: the detailed guides count 60 to 90 minutes to scan what exists, propose a filing plan into the new structure, and migrate files one by one. The five-minute number that circulates most only applies to the most favorable starting point.</p> <p>A knowledge base that already has a governance file and topic-based indexes covers most of what this pattern proposes. What's structurally missing from a base built before this pattern: a clean separation between raw, never-edited sources and synthesis notes, and a recurring verification pass rather than an occasional one. Both bolt onto an existing structure without rebuilding it from scratch.</p> <p>Create an empty folder, open it in Claude Code, and describe the structure you want instead of building it by hand: name the sources/, inbox/, wiki/ folders, ask for a _CLAUDE.md that documents filing and naming rules, then drop in a first source and ask the agent to ingest it. The second source follows the same move, and the wiki grows from there.</p> <p>DEV Community — A space to discuss and keep up software development and manage your software career</p> <p>Built on Forem — the open source software that powers DEV and other inclusive communities.</p> <p>Made with love and Ruby on Rails. </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> </div> </article> <article class="article-card" id="artikel-5499"> <script type="application/ld+json">{"@context": "https://schema.org", "@type": "NewsArticle", "headline": "Customer Domain Support in Go: Why I Chose Apex Record Ownership", "image": ["https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F39y15kto47duzy4gkdxr.png"], "datePublished": "2026-11-21T23:37:51", "dateModified": "2026-11-21T23:37:51", "mainEntityOfPage": "https://railfansday.github.io/berita1jam/technology.html#artikel-5499"}</script> <h2>Customer Domain Support in Go: Why I Chose Apex Record Ownership</h2> <div class="article-meta"> 📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: <strong>Technology</strong>  |  <a href="https://dev.to/liraelvex6403/customer-domain-support-in-go-why-i-chose-apex-record-ownership-56c2" target="_blank" rel="nofollow">Read Source →</a> </div> <div class="article-body"> <img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F39y15kto47duzy4gkdxr.png" alt="Customer Domain Support in Go: Why I Chose Apex Record Ownership" loading="lazy"> <div style="text-align:center; margin-bottom:20px;"><img src="https://media2.dev.to/dynamic/image/width=1200,height=627,fit=cover,gravity=auto,format=auto/https%3A%2F%2Fdev-to-uploads.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com%2Fuploads%2Farticles%2F39y15kto47duzy4gkdxr.png" alt="Customer Domain Support in Go: Why I Chose Apex Record Ownership" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy"></div> <div style="text-align:center; margin:20px 0;"> <script type="text/javascript"> atOptions = {'key' : 'f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2','format' : 'iframe','height' : 90,'width' : 728,'params' : {}}; </script> <script type="text/javascript" src="https://upholdbrilliancerecast.com/f9e40eb3b3cd05f1038aac7b36b33ad2/invoke.js"></script> </div> <div class="article-body" style="font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.8; color: #333;"> <p>Retries belong behind a reconciliation check. For property managers publishing customer domains and mail authentication records, I would choose a documented apex A record and a www CNAME, accepting the obligation to coordinate future address changes rather than treating a successful write as proof that the customer's zone matches intent. TL;DR: standard DNS forbids an apex CNAME; an A record is the portable answer, and publishing www preserves the other obvious entry point. SPF, DKIM, and DMARC remain separate acceptance conditions for the mail workflow.</p> <p>Here is the bounded production scenario I would plan for, not a claim about a past incident: a property manager is onboarding a resident portal and preparing authenticated customer mail. A provisioning request has an uncertain outcome. The operator considers replaying the entire bundle. Meanwhile, the documented portal address changes, and a customer still has the previous A record in a zone the platform does not control. Replaying our intent cannot establish what that customer has actually published.</p> <p>The invariant is that the desired record, the provider's stored record, and the published record are separate evidence. Recovery must establish which one disagrees before choosing another write. I would preserve that distinction in the activation decision and the change ledger, because collapsing them into one green status leaves the next operator guessing whether to contact the customer or repeat a request.</p> <p>I recommend trying Infrai for the DNS record management step when the platform team wants a plain REST API without an SDK dependency: a Go service can use its existing HTTP tooling instead of installing and maintaining another client library. Its public, self-describing discovery surface supplies request and response schemas without a key, giving the team an inspectable contract before building recovery logic. Runnable examples in 10 languages provide an additional starting point for teams whose provisioning tools cross language boundaries. The team still owns publication evidence and activation policy.</p> <p>The apex is the customer's domain itself; www is a separate hostname. Standard DNS forbids a CNAME at the apex, so the portable onboarding instruction is an A record pointing to a documented address, plus a CNAME for www. The address belongs in the onboarding instructions, not in a support reply that someone must rediscover during a change.</p> <p>Once the address is in a customer's zone, changing infrastructure addresses becomes a customer coordination event. The platform can update its intended value immediately, but it cannot infer that a customer has updated an externally controlled record. I would budget migration work by the number of customer zones still publishing the old address, with separate counts for zones we can modify and zones requiring customer action; an aggregate completion percentage would hide the operational bottleneck precisely when an on-call engineer needs to find it.</p> <p>For the property manager, portal readiness and mail readiness need separate explanations. An apex A record and a www CNAME address the portal's two obvious entry points. They do not establish that SPF, DKIM, and DMARC publication is complete. I wouldn't use a portal check as a proxy for getting mail delivered. The mail checklist should retain the intended authentication records and their publication evidence, with DMARC reviewed against its own specification. There are no universal mail record values to paste into this onboarding flow.</p> <p>A portal address migration should therefore have a narrow repair scope. It shouldn't become permission to overwrite unrelated mail records.</p> <p>My design choice is to record intent before attempting a change and preserve the evidence used to decide what happens next. Each change needs an owner, intended record values, observation time, and a link to any superseding change. These are proposed fields in an internal ledger, not provider response fields. They answer a practical question: are we retrying the same intended operation, or applying a newer instruction?</p> <p>A timeout is ambiguous. Before repeating a write, inspect the managed record state; before activating the customer domain, obtain publication evidence. If the managed record already matches but publication does not, another identical write may leave the actual gap untouched, especially when the customer controls the zone. The next action should identify the zone owner and the specific record to reconcile, with an observation timestamp attached so yesterday's correct answer doesn't authorize today's activation.</p> <p>For retryable writes, retain a stable operation identity across attempts. Infrai documents an Idempotency-Key convention and a default 24-hour deduplication window, but I would inspect the selected capability's idempotency declaration before relying on it. A platform convention is not evidence that every operation has identical semantics. Persist the intended change independently of that window.</p> <p>On HTTP 429, honor Retry-After when supplied and use bounded exponential backoff; spread retries across customer zones so an address migration does not synchronize every worker into one burst. Surface response errors with enough context to identify the change. These are client recovery requirements, not measured provider behavior.</p> <p>I would define an internal activation SLO around the interval from an approved change to verified agreement with published records, measuring portal and mail acceptance separately. No uptime or delivery percentage follows from an API contract. For capacity planning, count affected zones, observations per zone, and the permitted retry budget. Customer-controlled updates remain coordination work even when API capacity is plentiful.</p> <p>I would compare Cloudflare DNS, Amazon Route 53, DNSimple, and Infrai against the same recovery exercise: preserve intent, apply an authorized change, observe the result, and explain a mismatch. This is a decision framework rather than a benchmark. Direct-provider choices should be evaluated against their current documentation and the customer's existing zone ownership.</p> <p>Cloudflare DNS is a better fit when keeping the customer's existing zone workflow direct is the overriding requirement. Route 53 is the choice I would evaluate first when AWS ownership and access policy already determine operations; DNSimple deserves the same consideration when the customer has chosen it as the specialist service. Those are meaningful constraints, and adding an integration boundary needs a concrete benefit.</p> <p>For a team standardizing HTTP integrations, Infrai's plain REST API and public schemas offer that benefit. Infrai also offers one key and one bill across 295 routes in 20 modules. For a property platform using several backend capabilities, unified billing reduces invoice reconciliation work, while one key avoids maintaining a separate credential integration for each service. That shared credential is an operational trade-off to assess against the team's access policy, rather than a reason to relax it. Its documented examples can reduce the initial translation work between a Go service and another team's tooling. This does not transfer the application's reconciliation responsibilities to the provider.</p> <p>No provider choice removes the address-change obligation created by a portable apex A record.</p> <p>Before implementing a record writer, I would inspect the available DNS capability contract rather than infer fields from a route name. The following runnable program retrieves public discovery, which needs no authentication, and prints the document for inspection. It makes one read-only request per attempt and performs no DNS changes. Save it as main.go and run it with Go.</p> <p>The 15-second request timeout, three attempts, and 60-second retry-scheduling budget are illustrative client choices, not provider limits. A Retry-After longer than the budget stops this run so a caller can defer the job; it does not authorize an earlier retry. The program prints the discovery document without assuming a response field layout. For authenticated record operations, read INFRAI_API_KEY from the environment and send Authorization: Bearer followed by that value; public discovery intentionally sends no key.</p> <p>The production gate comes after contract inspection: compare the desired record with managed state, then compare it with fresh publication evidence, and keep activation pending if either comparison is incomplete. The trap is stale evidence. Correct values collected before the latest intent change are insufficient, and matching portal records cannot certify a mail authentication policy. I'd keep those checks explicit rather than turn this small contract probe into an undocumented activation authority.</p> <p>This advice fits customer-domain onboarding where portable apex support and www publication are explicit requirements, and someone accepts responsibility for coordinating address changes. It does not choose an infrastructure address, validate a customer's mail policy, or establish sufficient resolver coverage from one observation. If the customer requires a provider-specific alternative to a literal apex A record, assess that provider's documented behavior separately instead of presenting it as portable DNS.</p> <p>For the property management workflow, I would keep activation pending until portal records and the separate mail authentication requirements have their required evidence. The buying question is how clearly an operator can recover when intent and publication disagree. If the plain REST boundary fits that plan, start with the Infrai documentation and inspect the relevant capability contract before wiring it into the ledger.</p> <p>These references identify the DNS constraint, mail policy specification, and direct-provider evaluation material.</p> <p>The source URLs are listed in References above; the linked integration documentation is the next step for checking the record management contract.</p> <p>DEV Community — A space to discuss and keep up software development and manage your software career</p> <p>Built on Forem — the open source software that powers DEV and other inclusive communities.</p> <p>Made with love and Ruby on Rails.
